On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against No Spring, no build tool, no database.\n\n---\n\n## The short version\n\n```sh\n./build.sh              # compile and package build/session-cookie-auth-demo.war\n./run-demo.sh           # start a throwaway Tomcat and walk through one whole sign-in with curl\n./tests/unit-checks.sh  # 67 checks on the parts that need no server\n./tests/end-to-end.sh   # 69 checks against the real server, driven by curl\n```\n\nThen open `http://localhost:8181/app/login` (the port is `8181` unless you pass `HTTP_PORT`) and sign\nin as:\n\n| Username | Password           | Roles        |\n|----------|--------------------|--------------|\n| `alice`  | `Alice!2345`       | admin, user  |\n| `bob`    | `B0b-the-Builder`  | user         |\n\n---\n\n## Where the code is\n\nEverything is under `src/main/java`. Nothing is generated; these are the files.\n\n| File | What it is for |\n|------|----------------|\n| `com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java` | Shows the sign-in form, checks the credentials, issues the cookie. **The heart of the answer to this task.** |\n| `com/example/auth/web/AccountServlet.java` | A page that only renders when the cookie names a live session |\n| `com/example/auth/web/AuthFilter.java` | The gate in front of every page that needs an identity |\n| `com/example/auth/web/LogoutServlet.java` | Ends the session and clears the cookie |\n| `com/example/auth/web/StatusServlet.java` | Says how the application is configured, as JSON |\n| `com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java` | Reading and writing the one cookie this app cares about |\n| `com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java` | Builds the collaborators at startup from `web.xml` settings |\n| `com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java` | Counts failed attempts and locks a caller out for a while |\n| `com/example/auth/PasswordService.java` | Turns passwords into verifiable records and back |\n| `com/example/auth/SessionStore.java` | Session records: which cookie belongs to whom, and until when |\n| `com/example/auth/UserStore.java`, `InMemoryUserStore.java`, `AuthenticatedUser.java` | The accounts, behind an interface |\n\nSupporting files:\n\n- `src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml` — settings: password cost, session lifetimes, lockout, accounts\n- `build.sh` — compiles and packages the war\n- `run-demo.sh` — starts Tomcat and narrates one sign-in with `curl`\n- `tests/java/UnitChecks.java`, `tests/unit-checks.sh` — checks that need no server, no port, no network\n- `tests/end-to-end.sh` — the checks against the running server, below\n- `tools/tomcat-env.sh` — Tomcat start/stop plumbing shared by the two scripts above\n\n---\n\n## What happens, step by step\n\n1. **`GET /login`.** The server makes up a random 32-byte identifier, writes it as the `app_session`\n   cookie, and remembers *nothing* about who is signed in — the record behind that cookie is\n   anonymous and exists only to hold a single-use token that is embedded in the form.\n\n2. **`POST /login` with the form.** The server checks the token, then looks up the account, re-runs\n   the password stretching with that account's own salt and cost, and compares the two digests in a\n   way whose duration does not depend on where the first differing byte is.\n\n3. **On success:** the anonymous record and its identifier are thrown away, a **brand-new** random\n   identifier is generated, the identity is stored against it, and it is sent to the browser as\n   `Set-Cookie: app_session=...; Path=/app; HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax`. The browser is redirected to\n   `/account`.\n\n   The identifier that existed *before* the credentials were proven never survives the login. That\n   single rule is what stops session fixation: an attacker who tricks a victim into starting with a\n   known cookie value finds that value discarded the moment the victim signs in.\n\n4. **`GET /account` later.** The browser sends the cookie automatically. `AuthFilter` looks the value\n   up in `SessionStore`; a hit means the request continues, a miss means a redirect back to the\n   sign-in page (or a `401` with a JSON body, when the caller is a script rather than a browser\n   navigating a page). Nothing about identity is read from any other request header, parameter, or\n   hidden form field — the cookie is the whole story.\n\n5. **`POST /logout`.** The server deletes the record and re-issues the cookie with `Max-Age=0`, so\n   the browser drops it. Even a copy of the old value is now worthless: the server has nothing to\n   match it against.\n\n---\n\n## What the cookie looks like on the wire\n\n```\nSet-Cookie: app_session=tPqI_q5Q52lb7k58amqwHtMwiTV0FjZrPhi6uHJ3oG8; Path=/app; HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax\n```\n\n- **`HttpOnly`** — page script cannot read it, so a cross-site scripting bug on some other page of\n  the site cannot simply copy the session out.\n- **`SameSite=Lax`** — a form posted from somebody else's site does not carry it, which kills most\n  cross-site request forgery before it starts.\n- **`Secure`** — added automatically whenever the request arrived over HTTPS.\n- **`Path=/app`** — sent only to this application, not to every application on the host.\n- **No `Expires`/`Max-Age`** by default — a \"session cookie\" in the browser's own sense: closing the\n  browser ends it.\n- **`Max-Age`** only when \"Keep me signed in\" is ticked, and then with a lifetime the *server* decided,\n  never longer than the absolute session timeout.\n\nThe value is 43 Base64url characters: 256 bits from `SecureRandom`. It is not derived from the\nusername or the time, and it is never printed inside a page — which the tests check, because\n`HttpOnly` is worth little if the application helpfully writes the identifier into the HTML.\n\n---\n\n## Decisions worth explaining\n\n**Sessions are kept server-side, in `SessionStore`, not in the container's `HttpSession`.** The\ncookie carries an opaque identifier and nothing else; the identity lives in memory on the server,\nkeyed by it. It also means the parts worth testing — expiry, session-fixation protection, the lockout\n— can be tested at all. A real deployment would put these records in Redis or a database so several\nservers can share them.\n\n**Passwords are stretched with PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, 240 000 iterations, 16-byte random salt per\naccount,** and stored as `pbkdf2-sha256$iterations$base64(salt)$base64(digest)`. Each record carries\nits own salt and cost, so raising the number later does not break existing accounts: an old record\nstill verifies at its own cost. What the format makes possible — re-storing an account at the new cost\nthe next time its owner signs in — this demo does not do, because it has no account to write back to;\nsee \"What this is not\". The comparison uses `MessageDigest.isEqual`.\n\n**Some passwords are refused rather than stored, because the JDK cannot tell them apart from other\npasswords.** Measured on this JDK's `PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256`: it throws away NUL bytes at the end of the\ncharacter array, so `abc` and `abc`-followed-by-a-NUL stretch to the *same* digest; and it encodes an\nunpaired surrogate half as a `?`, so `p` plus half of an emoji and `p?` stretch to the same digest too.\nSomeone allowed to store `abc<NUL>` would find that `abc` opens their account. So `PasswordService`\nrejects the ambiguous shapes — empty, trailing NUL, unpaired surrogate half — on the way *in*\n(`hash()` refuses to store them) and refuses them on the way out (`verify()` will not accept them),\nwhile leaving alone the shapes that do survive the stretch, such as a NUL in the middle of a password.\nA refused attempt is not a shortcut: it is charged a full stretching run whose result is thrown away,\nso a rejected sign-in takes the same time whichever reason caused the rejection. Delete the one\ncondition in `verify()` and `abc<NUL>` verifies against `abc`'s record — the unit checks pin that down.\n\n**A wrong password and an unknown username answer identically**, and the unknown-username path still\npays a full stretching cost. Without that, the endpoint answers \"is this username taken?\" for\nfree — either from the wording or from a stopwatch. `./tests/end-to-end.sh` times both paths and\nfails if they diverge.\n\n**Failures are counted.** Five rejections for one username within 15 minutes earns a 15-minute\nlockout for that caller and username together, with a `429` and a `Retry-After` header — and the\nlockout refuses even a *correct* password, because its job is to slow the guesser down, not to be\npolite.\n\n**Everything is denied by default.** `AuthFilter` covers `/*` and lets through only `/login`,\n`/logout`, `/status` and `/favicon.ico`. A new endpoint is behind the gate the moment it exists,\nrather than after somebody remembers to add it to a list.\n\n**No `jsessionid` in URLs.** `web.xml` sets `tracking-mode` to `COOKIE` only. A session identifier in\na link leaks into browser history, proxy logs and `Referer` headers.\n\n**Output is escaped, not assembled.** Usernames and messages pass through `Html.escape` before they\nreach a page, and the JSON status body goes through `Json.quote`.\n\n---\n\n## How to run it\n\n### Build\n\n```sh\n./build.sh\n```\n\nNeeds a JDK 17 or newer and a Servlet 6.0 (`jakarta.*`) API jar. The jar is *found*, not hardcoded:\n`SERVLET_API_JAR=/path/to/jar ./build.sh` wins if set, otherwise `build.sh` looks in the usual Tomcat\nplaces. It refuses a jar that has no `jakarta/servlet/http/Cookie.class` in it — a Tomcat 9 jar sits\nat the same path and compiles nothing useful, because it still carries the old `javax.*` namespace.\n\nIt compiles with `-Xlint:all -Werror`, so a warning fails the build. Sources are ASCII-only, so the\nbuild needs no `-encoding` flag on any console. The compiled classes are left in `build/classes`,\nwhich is what the unit checks run against.\n\n### Check it\n\n```sh\n./tests/unit-checks.sh   # quick, no server\n./tests/end-to-end.sh    # builds, deploys, drives a throwaway Tomcat\n```\n\n### Try it\n\n```sh\n./run-demo.sh\n```\n\nDeploys the war into a **throwaway** Tomcat under `build/tomcat` (your Tomcat installation is never\ntouched), then prints the whole exchange: the settings, the refusal without a cookie, the cookie the\nsign-in page sets, the `302` on success, the protected page, the sign-out, and the same request\nrefused afterwards. Add `--keep-open` to leave the server running so you can poke at it.\n\n```\nTOMCAT_HOME=/opt/tomcat HTTP_PORT=9090 ./run-demo.sh\n```\n\nTo use a particular Tomcat or port. If a port is taken, the script says so rather than borrowing it\nsilently — and it only counts a port as taken when something is *listening* on it, which matters\nbecause a port held by yesterday's closed connections looks busy to a careless check for minutes.\n\nOr deploy `build/session-cookie-auth-demo.war` into your own container's deployment directory.\n\n### Accounts\n\nSeeded from `web.xml`, `seed.user.<name>` parameters, format `password | role,role`. Stretched at\nstartup and held in memory; gone on restart. These are throwaway credentials for a demo, and the\nshape of the thing is what matters: in a real deployment the accounts come from a database and\nnothing password-shaped appears in a deployment descriptor.\n\n---\n\n## What the tests actually check\n\nThere are two suites, and they check different kinds of claim.\n\n**`./tests/unit-checks.sh`** (67 checks) compiles `tests/java/UnitChecks.java` against the classes\n`build.sh` produced and runs it in a plain JVM — no Tomcat, no port, no network. It covers the claims\nthat are awkward to observe from `curl`: which password shapes the JDK would silently merge together,\nthat a stored record round-trips through its text form and that a stray character appended to it is\nrejected rather than quietly decoded, that a record never prints its own salt or digest, that sessions\nexpire on schedule and that a persistent session keeps its absolute deadline when it is touched, that a\nform token cannot be spent twice, and the lockout arithmetic — including that a lockout leaves a\ndifferent username, or a different caller, alone.\n\n**`./tests/end-to-end.sh`** (69 checks) builds the war, deploys it into a throwaway Tomcat, drives it\nwith `curl` and asserts on the bytes that came back — status line, `Set-Cookie` attributes, `Location`,\nbody, elapsed time. It reasons about nothing; if the container disagrees with what the code appears to\nsay, the test finds out. Both suites pass, and both are stable across repeated runs.\n\nHighlights from the end-to-end run:\n\n- `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax` and `Path=/app` are really on the `Set-Cookie` line the container sent.\n- The identifier that survives login differs from the one the sign-in page was given.\n- An identifier of the right shape that this server never issued gets bounced.\n- The same identifier, replayed by hand after sign-out, still gets bounced.\n- Two browsers signed in as the same user are independent: signing one out leaves the other working.\n- \"Keep me signed in\" produces a `Max-Age` inside the 8-hour ceiling.\n- A wrong password and a nonexistent username return the same status, the same message, **and the\n  same duration** — and a rejected attempt really does spend the stretching time, so the timing\n  check cannot be satisfied by a stub.\n- A form token posted twice is honoured once.\n- `GET /logout` is refused with `405`: a link or an `<img>` tag cannot sign anybody out.\n- After five rejections, the *correct* password is refused too, with `429` and `Retry-After`.\n- Neither the session identifier nor any account detail appears in a page or in the public status\n  output.\n\n---\n\n## What this is not\n\nA framework, or something to deploy as-is. What it would need before facing the internet:\n\n- **HTTPS, really** — a TLS terminator in front, `Secure` cookies, HSTS, HTTP refused.\n- **A real account store** — a database, with an upsert-and-upgrade path, instead of `web.xml`.\n- **Shared session storage** — Redis or similar, so requests can land on any server, plus a way to\n  revoke every session for one user.\n- **A modern password hash** — Argon2id or scrypt if a vetted library is available; the per-record\n  cost field is already there so records can migrate as the algorithm changes.\n- **Rate limiting at the edge**, account-recovery flows, an audit trail of sign-ins, and a Content\n  Security Policy once there is real page script to govern.\n\nThe session cookie handling itself is the part worth reading; the rest is scaffolding to make it\nrunnable and testable as one thing.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"6a321063-7d72-4ded-80b5-59f93286f385","session_id":"45c8944e-d3f6-41a6-ac2d-c70f0846fd52","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_43d8c3c8a8fb4d2e9f534d89","is_error":false,"content":"package com.example.auth;\n\nimport java.security.GeneralSecurityException;\nimport java.security.MessageDigest;\nimport java.security.SecureRandom;\nimport java.util.Arrays;\nimport java.util.Base64;\nimport java.util.Objects;\nimport javax.crypto.SecretKeyFactory;\nimport javax.crypto.spec.PBEKeySpec;\n\n/**\n * Turns passwords into verifiable records and back again.\n *\n * <p>Passwords are never stored or compared in plain form. Each password is stretched with\n * PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 under a fresh random salt, and verification re-runs the same stretching and\n * compares the digests in constant time.\n */\npublic final class PasswordService {\n\n    static final String PBKDF2_SHA256 = \"pbkdf2-sha256\";\n    private static final String KEY_FACTORY_ALGORITHM = \"PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256\";\n    private static final int SALT_BYTES = 16;\n    private static final int HASH_BYTES = 32;\n    static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000;\n    static final int MAX_ITERATIONS = 5_000_000;\n\n    private final SecureRandom random = new SecureRandom();\n    private final int iterations;\n\n    /**\n     * A syntactically valid, unreachable password record. Logging in with a name that does not\n     * exist spends this record's stretching time, so a rejected guess costs the same as a wrong\n     * one and \"is this username taken?\" cannot be answered by stopwatch.\n     */\n    private final StoredPassword timingReference;\n\n    public PasswordService(int iterations) {\n        if (iterations < MIN_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"iterations must be between \" + MIN_ITERATIONS\n                    + \" and \" + MAX_ITERATIONS + \", was \" + iterations);\n        }\n        this.iterations = iterations;\n        this.timingReference = new StoredPassword(PBKDF2_SHA256, iterations, randomBytes(SALT_BYTES),\n                new byte[HASH_BYTES]);\n    }\n\n    public int iterations() {\n        return iterations;\n    }\n\n    /** The name of the stretching function, as it appears in a stored record. */\n    public String algorithm() {\n        return PBKDF2_SHA256;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Whether stretching this candidate can be trusted to mean exactly what it says. Three shapes\n     * are refused, because the key factory quietly maps each of them onto a different password --\n     * and two passwords that stretch to the same digest are the same password, whoever typed them:\n     *\n     * <ul>\n     *   <li>nothing at all, which the factory would happily stretch;</li>\n     *   <li>a trailing NUL, which the factory drops: {@code \"abc\"} and {@code \"abc\\0\"} come out\n     *       identical (a leading or interior NUL is fine, those do survive);</li>\n     *   <li>an unpaired surrogate, which the factory encodes as a literal {@code ?}: {@code \"p\"}\n     *       plus a lone high surrogate, a lone low surrogate and a plain {@code \"p?\"} all collide.</li>\n     * </ul>\n     *\n     * <p>Measured against this JDK's {@code PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256}, not assumed.\n     */\n    public static boolean isUsableCandidate(char[] password) {\n        if (password == null || password.length == 0) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        if (password[password.length - 1] == '\\0') {\n            return false;\n        }\n        for (int i = 0; i < password.length; i++) {\n            char c = password[i];\n            if (Character.isHighSurrogate(c)) {\n                if (i + 1 >= password.length || !Character.isLowSurrogate(password[i + 1])) {\n                    return false;\n                }\n                i++; // the low half of the pair just consumed\n            } else if (Character.isLowSurrogate(c)) {\n                return false; // a low half with no high half in front of it\n            }\n        }\n        return true;\n    }\n\n    /** Stretches a password with a new random salt. The caller's array is left untouched. */\n    public StoredPassword hash(char[] password) {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(password, \"password\");\n        if (!isUsableCandidate(password)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"this password cannot be stored without ambiguity\");\n        }\n        byte[] salt = randomBytes(SALT_BYTES);\n        byte[] digest = derive(password, salt, iterations);\n        if (digest == null) {\n            throw new IllegalStateException(KEY_FACTORY_ALGORITHM + \" is unavailable in this JVM\");\n        }\n        return new StoredPassword(PBKDF2_SHA256, iterations, salt, digest);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Re-stretches {@code password} against {@code stored}. Returns {@code false} for a wrong\n     * password, for a record this service could not have produced, and for a candidate whose shape\n     * {@link #isUsableCandidate} refuses -- a candidate that cannot mean one thing cannot prove\n     * anything.\n     */\n    public boolean verify(char[] password, StoredPassword stored) {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(password, \"password\");\n        if (stored == null || !isUsableCandidate(password)) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        byte[] digest = derive(password, stored.salt(), stored.iterations());\n        try {\n            return digest != null && MessageDigest.isEqual(digest, stored.hash());\n        } finally {\n            if (digest != null) {\n                Arrays.fill(digest, (byte) 0);\n            }\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Pays the stretching cost of a real attempt for an attempt that is going to be refused anyway --\n     * unknown username, malformed username, a candidate shape that cannot be verified. Without it,\n     * \"the server gave up before touching the store\" is answerable with a stopwatch, and that answer\n     * narrows what is worth guessing.\n     *\n     * <p>The candidate itself is never used as a credential here; it is stretched against a fixed\n     * unreachable record and the result is thrown away.\n     */\n    public void chargeRefusedAttempt(char[] password) {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(password, \"password\");\n        derive(password, timingReference.salt(), timingReference.iterations());\n    }\n\n    private static byte[] derive(char[] password, byte[] salt, int iterations) {\n        PBEKeySpec spec = new PBEKeySpec(password, salt, iterations, HASH_BYTES * 8);\n        try {\n            SecretKeyFactory factory = SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(KEY_FACTORY_ALGORITHM);\n            return factory.generateSecret(spec).getEncoded();\n        } catch (GeneralSecurityException | IllegalArgumentException e) {\n            // Fail closed: an unusable key factory or a rejected spec must never look like a match.\n            return null;\n        } finally {\n            spec.clearPassword();\n        }\n    }\n\n return new Attempt(failures + 1, firstFailureAt, null);\n        }\n\n        Attempt lockedUntil(Instant until) {\n            return new Attempt(0, null, until);\n        }\n    }\n\n    private return window;\n    }\n\n    public Duration lockout() {\n        return lockout;\n    }\n\n    /** Drops counters nobody has touched recently, so the map cannot grow without bound. */\n    private Everything is driven from the environment:\n#\n#   TOMCAT_HOME   Tomcat install to run from   (default: newest /tmp/apache-tomcat-* on disk)\n#   HTTP_PORT     where to listen              (default: 8181)\n#   SHUTDOWN_PORT where `stop` is accepted     (default: 8109)\n#\n# Nothing here hardcodes a machine: the Tomcat install is discovered, and the Servlet API jar used\n# for compiling is discovered by build.sh.\n#\n# shellcheck shell=sh\n\n# Callers set PROJECT_DIR before sourcing (its own location is not reliably reachable from /bin/sh\n# once a file has been sourced rather than executed); the fallback covers a direct `. ./tools/...`.\nPROJECT_DIR=\"${PROJECT_DIR:-$(CDPATH= cd -- \"$(dirname -- \"$0\")/..\" && pwd)}\"\nBUILD_DIR=\"$PROJECT_DIR/build\"\nCATALINA_BASE=\"$BUILD_DIR/tomcat\"\nWAR=\"$BUILD_DIR/session-cookie-auth-demo.war\"\nAPP_CONTEXT=\"app\"\nHTTP_PORT=\"${HTTP_PORT:-8181}\"\nHTTPS_PORT=\"${HTTPS_PORT:-8643}\"\nSHUTDOWN_PORT=\"${SHUTDOWN_PORT:-8109}\"\nBASE_URL=\"http://127.0.0.1:$HTTP_PORT/$APP_CONTEXT\"\nSECURE_URL=\"https://127.0.0.1:$HTTPS_PORT/$APP_CONTEXT\"\n\nfind_tomcat_home() {\n  if [ -n \"${TOMCAT_HOME:-}\" ]; then\n    echo \"$TOMCAT_HOME\"\n    return\n  fi\n  for candidate in /tmp/apache-tomcat-* /opt/tomcat /usr/local/tomcat; do\n    if [ -x \"$candidate/bin/catalina.sh\" ] \\\n        && jar --list --file \"$candidate/lib/servlet-api.jar\" 2>/dev/null \\\n             | grep -q '^jakarta/servlet/http/Cookie.class$'; then\n      echo \"$candidate\"\n      return\n    fi\n  done\n  echo \"\"\n}\n\n# A JVM on this box resolves user.home from the passwd entry, not from $HOME, so nothing in this\n# demo is isolated by overriding HOME. Everything it can write goes under CATALINA_BASE instead.\nscaffold_catalina_base() {\n  rm -rf \"$CATALINA_BASE\"\n  mkdir -p \"$CATALINA_BASE/conf/Catalina/localhost\" \"$CATALINA_BASE/logs\" \\\n           \"$CATALINA_BASE/webapps\" \"$CATALINA_BASE/work\" \"$CATALINA_BASE/temp\"\n  cp \"$TOMCAT_HOME/conf/web.xml\" \"$CATALINA_BASE/conf/web.xml\"\n  cp \"$TOMCAT_HOME/conf/logging.properties\" \"$CATALINA_BASE/conf/logging.properties\"\n  # A throwaway self-signed certificate, generated fresh each time, so the HTTPS path can be driven\n  # and checked rather than argued about: the cookie's Secure attribute is only observable over TLS.\n  # Without this connector the application would always answer \"not secure\" and the Secure flag would\n  # silently never appear.\n  local keystore=\"$CATALINA_BASE/demo.jks\"\n  keytool -genkeypair -alias demo -keyalg RSA -keysize 2048 \\\n          -dname \"CN=localhost, O=throwaway demo, C=US\" \\\n          -validity 2 -storepass changeit -keypass changeit \\\n          -keystore \"$keystore\" -storetype PKCS12 >/dev/null 2>&1\n  if [ ! -f \"$keystore\" ]; then\n    echo \"warning: could not generate a throwaway keystore; the HTTPS checks will not run\" >&2\n  fi\n\n  # A real shutdown port, not -1: with -1, `catalina.sh stop` is a silent no-op and the leaked server\n  # then holds HTTP_PORT for the next run and reports its answers as this one's.\n  cat > \"$CATALINA_BASE/conf/server.xml\" <<XML\n<?xml version=\"1.0\" encoding=\"UTF-8\"?>\n<Server port=\"$SHUTDOWN_PORT\" shutdown=\"SHUTDOWN\">\n  <Listener className=\"org.apache.catalina.startup.VersionLoggerListener\"/>\n  <Listener className=\"org.apache.catalina.core.JreMemoryLeakPreventionListener\"/>\n  <Listener className=\"org.apache.catalina.mbeans.GlobalResourcesLifecycleListener\"/>\n  <GlobalNamingResources/>\n  <Service name=\"demo\">\n    <Executor name=\"demo\" maxThreads=\"32\" minSpareThreads=\"4\"/>\n    <Connector port=\"$HTTP_PORT\" protocol=\"HTTP/1.1\" executor=\"demo\"\n               connectionTimeout=\"20000\" redirectPort=\"$HTTPS_PORT\"/>\n    <Connector port=\"$HTTPS_PORT\" protocol=\"org.apache.coyote.http11.Http11NioProtocol\"\n               executor=\"demo\" SSLEnabled=\"true\" scheme=\"https\" secure=\"true\">\n      <SSLHostConfig>\n        <Certificate certificateKeystoreFile=\"$keystore\" certificateKeystorePassword=\"changeit\"\n                     type=\"RSA\"/>\n      </SSLHostConfig>\n    </Connector>\n    <Engine name=\"demo\" defaultHost=\"localhost\">\n      <Host name=\"localhost\" appBase=\"$CATALINA_BASE/webapps\" unpackWARs=\"true\" autoDeploy=\"true\">\n        <Valve className=\"org.apache.catalina.valves.AccessLogValve\" directory=\"logs\"\n               prefix=\"access\" suffix=\".log\" pattern=\"%h %l %u %t &quot;%r&quot; %s %b\"/>\n      </Host>\n    </Engine>\n  </Service>\n</Server>\nXML\n  # The war is deployed as /app by being named app.war: the context path is then non-empty, which is\n  # what exercises the contextPath handling in the redirects and cookie Path attribute.\n  cp \"$WAR\" \"$CATALINA_BASE/webapps/$APP_CONTEXT.war\"\n}\n\nport_in_use() {\n  # Only rows in LISTEN state (st == 0A) and only their LOCAL address count. Matching the whole line\n  # instead also matches the remote-address column of yesterday's closed connections to the same\n  # port, which reports every port as busy for minutes after a run.\n  local hex\n  hex=$(printf '%04X' \"$1\")\n  if command -v ss >/dev/null 2>&1; then\n    ss -ltn 2>/dev/null | grep -q \":$1 \"\n    return\n  fi\n  awk -v want=\"$hex\" 'NR > 1 && $4 == \"0A\" { split($2, parts, \":\"); if (parts[2] == want) found = 1 }\n                      END { exit !found }' /proc/net/tcp 2>/dev/null\n}\n\nstart_tomcat() {\n  TOMCAT_HOME=$(find_tomcat_home)\n  if [ -z \"$TOMCAT_HOME\" ]; then\n    echo \"error: no Tomcat 10+ with the jakarta.* Servlet API found.\" >&2\n    echo \"       set TOMCAT_HOME=/path/to/tomcat (Tomcat 10.1 or newer) and re-run.\" >&2\n    return 1\n  fi\n  [ -f \"$WAR\" ] || { echo \"error: $WAR missing - run ./build.sh first\" >&2; return 1; }\n\n  for port in \"$HTTP_PORT\" \"$HTTPS_PORT\" \"$SHUTDOWN_PORT\"; do\n    if port_in_use \"$port\"; then\n      echo \"error: port $port is already in use.\" >&2\n      echo \"       another demo is running, or a previous one did not shut down. Last log lines:\" >&2\n      tail -n 25 \"$CATALINA_OUT\" >&2 2>/dev/null\n      return 1\n    fi\n    attempt=$((attempt + 1))\n    sleep 1\n  done\n  echo \"error: $BASE_URL/status never answered. Last log lines:\" >&2\n  tail -n 25 \"$CATALINA_OUT\" >&2 2>/dev/null\n  return 1\n}\n\nstop_tomcat() {\n  [ -n \"${CATALINA_HOME:-}\" ] || return 0\n  \"$CATALINA_HOME/bin/catalina.sh\" stop 2>/dev/null >/dev/null || true\n  attempt=0\n  while [ \"$attempt\" -lt 30 ]; do\n    port_in_use \"$SHUTDOWN_PORT\" || break\n    attempt=$((attempt + 1))\n    sleep 1\n  done\n  if port_in_use \"$SHUTDOWN_PORT\" && [ -f \"$CATALINA_BASE/tomcat.pid\" ]; then\n    # Only this demo's own pid, never a pattern match that could hit an unrelated JVM.\n    kill \"$(cat \"$CATALINA_BASE/tomcat.pid\")\" 2>/dev/null || true\n  fi\n  return 0\n}\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"60b76f06-c0a0-434d-8f30-be7fbb8be2de","session_id":"45c8944e-d3f6-41a6-ac2d-c70f0846fd52","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"60b76f06-c0a0-434d-8f30-be7fbb8be2de","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Now let me read the tests files (UnitChecks.java, unit-checks.sh, end-to-end.sh) briefly — they're code in the folder. The relevant fired check: **path-normalisation bypass of the exact-match allow-list**. Tomcat decodes `%2F`, `%25`, `;` path params, and can append a trailing slash. If any open path can be reached via a *different* path string that the filter doesn't equal-match... that direction is safe (unmatched = protected = deny). The dangerous direction is a *protected* path reachable by a string that DOES match an open entry. `/status;/foo`? → servletPath \"/status\" (pathParams stripped by Tomcat), pathInfo null → open; and the mapped servlet is StatusServlet → only /status behaviour. A remote attacker can inject arbitrary 43-char Base64url keys into the form-token map (each GET /login with a fresh made-up Cookie: app_session=<random> creates a new map entry). Same for the *session* map? No — sessions map only gets entries on successful login with a server-generated id. But the **form-token map** accepts arbitrary attacker-supplied keys: `attempts`/`formTokens`. Each entry = one map node + a 43-char token + Instant → memory growth. Purged only after 10 min. An attacker can pump `GET /login` with random cookies → unbounded memory growth (bounded by 10-minute window, but at high request rates: 100k rps × 600s = 60M entries ≈ several GB → OOM). I have everything needed. Now write the report.\n\nFinal finding list with verified locations:\n\n**SEC-01 — Hardcoded plaintext credentials in the deployment descriptor** — High — CWE-798 — `src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:60-68`. Corroborating: run-demo.sh:73, 91; tests/end-to-end.sh (many). Impact: war contains admin password; anyone who can read the repo/war/jenkins artifact gets Domain-of-app admin. Fix: env var / vault, seed only if explicitly enabled, fail startup if absent.\n\nActually, should severity be High or Critical? The crypto-audit skill rates \"Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical\". But this is a demo app on localhost with throwaway creds, and the file itself says so. Honest calibration: I'll say **High**. That's defensible.\n\n**SEC-02 — Session cookie travels over cleartext HTTP; no transport binding, no HSTS** — High — CWE-614 (primary) / CWE-319 / CWE-326 — `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java:51-64` + `web.xml:75-81` + `tools/tomcat-env.sh:78-79`. The Secure flag is derived from `request.isSecure()` so over plain HTTP the session cookie is unencrypted and interceptable; the HTTP connector only has redirectPort (never redirects by itself), and there is no `<transport-guarantee>CONFIDENTIAL</transport-guarantee>` user-data-constraint and no HSTS. tests/end-to-end.sh even asserts this is deliberate (\"over plain HTTP the cookie is deliberately not marked Secure\"). Fix: user-data-constraint CONFIDENTIAL + always setSecure(true) + HSTS.\n\nHmm — the Secure flag logic is arguably correct behaviour for a demo (can't set Secure over http or the cookie is dropped). But the security consequence (no enforced TLS) is real: there is nothing that forces TLS, so an on-path attacker can simply keep the victim on HTTP (SSL stripping) and capture the cookie. That's the finding: absence of enforced transport (no transport-guarantee, no HSTS, no redirect) rather than the Secure attribute per se. I'll frame it that way and cite both. CWE-319 primary + CWE-614 for the missing container-level `<secure>` for the container cookie. Let me pick CWE-319 as primary (\"Cleartext Transmission of Sensitive Information\") and mention CWE-614, CWE-326. Severity High.\n\n**SEC-03 — Unauthenticated password-stretching DoS (no request-rate limit on /login)** — High — CWE-400 / CWE-770 — `LoginServlet.java:101-108` + `web.xml:18-19` (240000 iterations). Every POST /login costs the server a full 240k PBKDF2 even for unknown users and empty passwords, and the only gate before it is a form token anyone can obtain with one cheap `GET /login`. Executor caps at 32 threads (tomcat-env.sh:77) so ~32 concurrent stretched attempts saturate the app; the throttle only counts *failures* per (ip,username), so a spray of distinct usernames or a single request per key never trips it. Rate-limiting skill: `\"auth\": {\"login\": {\"requests\": 5, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"ip\"}}` + global per_ip. Severity High (easy, unauthenticated, full outage).\n\nWait: does throttle count failures only? Yes: `recordFailure` on bad credentials; `verdictFor` only checks. So an attacker sending *valid* tokens with random usernames gets unlimited full-cost stretches. Also the empty-password path pays a full stretch. Confirmed at 101-108.\n\nAlso note LoginThrottle.verdictFor mutates attempts map — but purge + ConcurrentHashMap, fine.\n\nAlso: form token requires the pre-login session cookie; attacker just does GET /login (no cookie needed) → server creates a pre-login record. That also means unbounded SessionStore growth (memory DoS) — same family; mention within SEC-03 as an amplifier. SessionStore has a reaper + MAX_FORM_TOKEN_TTL 10m, so growth is bounded to ~10 min of requests. Still, memory growth per request for 10 min. Mention briefly.\n\n**SEC-04 — Response headers: no CSP/X-Frame-Options/X-Content-Type-Options/Referrer-Policy/HSTS on HTML** — Medium — CWE-693 (primary, \"Protection Mechanism Failure\") / CWE-1021 clickjacking / CWE-1023 — `Responses.java:23-33` (and 17-21). The HTML path sets Content-Type + cache headers only; the JSON path does set nosniff, proving the omission is HTML-only. No CSP means any residual XSS is fully exploitable (session theft via fetch — HttpOnly stops document.cookie but not authenticated requests); no X-Frame-Options/frame-ancestors → /account and /login are frameable → clickjacking (the login form is a CSRF-by-framing target); no HSTS (see SEC-02). Headers skill's own table lists exactly these as MISSING. Fix: set them in Responses.html (+ doNotCache or a filter).\n\n**SEC-05 — Log forging through the unvalidated username** — Medium — CWE-117 — `LoginServlet.java:113-115`. `username` is `trimToEmpty(getParameter(\"username\"))` — any length, any content including CR/LF and ANSI escapes — and it is logged on every rejected attempt, before/independent of `usernameValid()`. The USERNAME pattern at 36-37 is only used to decide whether to look the account up (line 97), never to sanitise the log value. Attacker-controlled newline injects forged log lines into catalina/localhost logs (or the access log pipeline if a collector parses them), letting them fake sign-ins for other accounts/IPs and hide theirs. Fix: log only after validation, or log the validated/canonical form + a truncation, and strip CR/LF.\n\n**SEC-06 — Login throttle keyed on (client IP, username) only** — Medium — CWE-307 — `LoginThrottle.java:66-68` + `LoginServlet.java:76`. Two consequences: (a) distributed per-account spray — one bad password per source IP per account never reaches 5, so `maxFailures=5` is bypassable with a botnet; the e2e test even asserts \"the lockout follows the account name\" with a single IP; (b) `getRemoteAddr()` behind a reverse proxy/LB is the proxy's address for everyone, so all users share one bucket → trivially self-inflicted lockout (a DoS of a named account: spray 5 bad ones from the shared IP... actually that locks everyone, which is an availability issue). No global/backoff layer, no edge limit. Fix: key on username alone for the account lockout + a separate per-IP request budget + trust X-Forwarded-For only from a configured proxy.\n\n**SEC-07 — `MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000` accepts downgraded password records** — Low — CWE-916 — `PasswordService.java:25` used at 39 and 174. The accepted floor for a stored record is 1 000 PBKDF2-SHA256 iterations (3 logs of magnitude below the 240 000 the app itself uses); the constructor and the record parser both accept it, so a record produced by an attacker-controlled import or a config typo (`auth.pbkdf2.iterations=1000`) is honoured, and an on-path/offline attacker can crack such a hash ~240× cheaper. tests/java/UnitChecks.java proves the refusal boundary is exactly 1 000. Fix: floor at or above current OWARM guidance (≥600 000 for PBKDF2-SHA-256 per OWASP Password Storage Cheat Sheet 2024) and transparently re-hash on login when a record's cost is below the live cost.\n\nCareful: the \"attacker-controlled import\" is speculative. Frame honestly: the floor is 240× below the deployment's own policy, permitting silent downgrade via a config typo or a migrated record, with no re-hash.\n\n**SEC-08 — Fixed keystore password `changeit` and cleartext secret in the generated server.xml** — Low — CWE-259 / CWE-798 / CWE-321 — `tools/tomcat-env.sh:59-62` and `:83-84`. The TLS private key is protected by a published default password, written into `conf/server.xml` in plaintext; the certificate is a 2-day self-signed throwaway. Fine for localhost, but the same helper is what a developer will copy toward a real environment. Fix: read from an env var/`$CATALINA_HOME`-side secret file, mark the file `chmod 600`, and refuse to generate if it's not a demo.\n\n**SEC-09 — Secrets and live session identifiers written to disk and exposed in process argv** — Low — CWE-313 / CWE-214 — `run-demo.sh:24, 52, 61-63, 72-74, 91`. `build/demo-cookies.txt` holds the issued 256-bit session id (line 68 `rm -f \"$JAR\"` deletes it only mid-script — and step 6 recreates a jar at 70 that is never removed), `build/demo-login.hdr` and `build/demo-bad.hdr` hold raw `Set-Cookie:` lines, and the password appears in the curl argv (visible in `ps`) and on stdout. These land under `build/` which is a normal artifact directory. Fix: keep scratch under `mktemp -d` (as end-to-end.sh already does) with a trap, use `--data @file`/stdin or `-K -` config instead of argv, and don't print the credential.\n\nHmm, need to be accurate about line 68 `rm -f \"$JAR\"`. From earlier grep: `24:JAR=...`, `25:rm -f \"$JAR\"`, `68:rm -f \"$JAR\"`, `70: <(curl -sS -c \"$JAR\" ...)`. So after line 68 the jar is deleted, then step 6 recreates it at line 70 and it is never cleaned. So at exit, `build/demo-cookies.txt` exists with a live session id — but the session was logged out? No: step 6 (line 70-74) is the *successful* login, step 8 (82-84) posts logout using `-c \"$JAR\"` which rewrites the jar with the cleared cookie. Hmm, line 83 uses `-b \"$JAR\" -c \"$JAR\"` so the jar gets updated to the cleared cookie. So the final jar state is post-logout. But if the user runs with KEEP_OPEN, the script leaves the server up... and the final jar has cleared cookie. Careful — overclaiming risk. The `demo-login.hdr`/`demo-bad.hdr` files DO retain live-at-the-time Set-Cookie session ids, and with `--keep-open`/interruption the jar retains a live one. Also line 61-65 `demo-bad.hdr` is from the failed attempt with a jar cookie (pre-login session id). Those are still valid pre-login session identifiers at rest.\n\nTo stay accurate, I'll say: session identifiers from the run are left in plaintext under `build/` (`demo-login.hdr`, `demo-bad.hdr`, and `demo-cookies.txt` unless the run reaches step 8), and the password is exposed in `ps` argv + terminal scrollback. Note that `build.sh` deletes `build/` on the next build, so exposure is until then. And the more durable problem is the credential in argv and printed on screen (line 91) plus in README. That's honest.\n\n**SEC-10 — `/status` publicly discloses authentication policy** — Low — CWE-200 — `StatusServlet.java:32-49`. Unauthenticated GET returns KDF algorithm, the exact iteration count, idle/absolute timeouts, cookie name/flags, and the lockout thresholds/window. None of it is secret by Kerckhoffs, but it hands an attacker the precise parameters to size a cracking budget (`240000` → cost per guess) and to time a lockout-evasion spray (5 per 15 m). The e2e test only asserts no *account names or hashes* leak. Fix: keep `status`/`health` booleans, move the policy detail behind admin authorisation.\n\nThat's 10 findings. Good number. Executive Summary\n\nThis is a hand-written Java 17 / Jakarta Servlet 6 demo that authenticates users with a server-side session store behind an `app_session` cookie, deployed as a WAR on Tomcat 10.1. Reviewed as a whole, the *application logic* is unusually careful: session ids are 256-bit `SecureRandom` values, session fixation is closed by invalidating the pre-login record and minting a fresh id, every secret comparison goes through `MessageDigest.isEqual`, a single-use 256-bit CSRF token is bound to the session record, the auth filter is default-deny over `/*`, and the login path deliberately spends the full PBKDF2 cost even for unknown usernames so the endpoint cannot be used to enumerate accounts. Section 6 lists these.\n\nThe findings are concentrated in the **deployment and configuration layer**, not the servlet code. The most serious issues are:\n\n- The deployment descriptor ships **plaintext passwords for an admin account** (`web.xml:60-68`), and they are repeated in the README, `run-demo.sh` and the end-to-end tests. Anything that can read the WAR — or the git history — holds a domain-admin-equivalent credential.\n- Nothing in the application or the generated Tomcat configuration **forces TLS**. The session cookie is marked `Secure` only \"if this particular request happened to arrive over TLS\", the plain HTTP connector is the one the demo and the tests actually use, and there is no HSTS anywhere. `tests/end-to-end.sh` asserts the insecure behaviour as intended (\"over plain HTTP the cookie is deliberately not marked Secure\"). Over any network an attacker can observe, the session id travels in cleartext.\n- `POST /login` performs **240,000 PBKDF2 iterations for every request from anyone**, and the only gate in front of it — the login throttle — is keyed on `(client IP, username)`. Because the username is attacker-chosen and never validated before the key is formed, an attacker gets an effectively **unbounded number of full-cost stretching operations** by varying the username, saturating the 32-thread executor with a handful of sockets and growing the throttle map without limit.\n\nEverything else is Medium or Low: absent browser-protection headers (clickjacking, MIME sniffing, no CSP), log injection through an unvalidated username, a lockout design that neither stops distributed spraying nor survives a reverse proxy, a PBKDF2 iteration floor of 1,000, and a set of demo-hygiene problems (hardcoded `changeit` keystore password, session ids and passwords written to disk and to the process command line, unauthenticated disclosure of the password and lockout policy).\n\nNone of these are exploitable *on localhost with no data behind them*, which is the demo's stated scope — but every one of them is a real vulnerability if the code is lifted into a deployment that carries anything worth stealing, and the README lists most of them as known gaps rather than as solved controls. Findings are reported at the severity they would have in that deployment.\n\n## 2. Scope\n\n| File | Purpose | Security-relevant behaviour |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml` | Deployment descriptor: all tunables as `context-param` | Sets PBKDF2 iterations (240000), idle/absolute session timeouts, lockout policy, and **two plaintext seed passwords including an `admin` account** (`:60-68`). Session config sets `http-only` and `tracking-mode COOKIE` but no `<transport-guarantee>` and no `<secure>` (`:75-81`) |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java` | Builds the app's singletons from init params | Parses the `seed.user.<name> = password \\| roles` params into the user store (`:88-108`); defaults iterations to 240 000 (`:57`) |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContextListener.java` | Context lifecycle hook | Constructs `AuthContext` at startup; fails fast if no seed account exists |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/SessionStore.java` | Server-side session records (the identity source of truth) | 256-bit ids, 256-bit per-session CSRF token, idle + absolute expiry, CAS `touch`, single-use token consumption, background reaper |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/InMemoryUserStore.java` | Username → stored-password/roles map | Clones and wipes `char[]` material; no persistence |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/PasswordService.java` | PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 hashing and verification | `MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000` (`:25`), `MAX_ITERATIONS = 5_000_000`; constant-time verify that zeroes the digest; `chargeRefusedAttempt` that does **not** (`:137-140`); strict record parsing |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/UserStore.java`, `AuthenticatedUser.java` | Interface and immutable principal | Role set is unmodifiable |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthFilter.java` | Default-deny gate on `/*` | `OPEN_PATHS = {/login, /logout, /status, /favicon.ico}` (`:36`); unknown paths are denied by default; refuses with JSON echoing the request path (`:74-76`) |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java` | Credential verification and session (re)mint | Throttle check → CSRF burn → lookup → equal-cost verify → `finishLogin`; logs the raw username on every rejection (`:113-115`); invalidates the pre-login session before minting (`:130-137`) |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LogoutServlet.java` | Ends the session | `POST` only, `GET` → 405 + `Allow`; deletes the record and clears the cookie; **no CSRF token check of its own** |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AccountServlet.java` | The protected page | Re-validates via `touch()` even though the filter already did; escapes all dynamic values |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/StatusServlet.java` | Unauthenticated status JSON | Publishes KDF algorithm, iteration count, session timeouts, cookie flags, and the full lockout policy (`:36-49`) |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java` | Brute-force throttle | Key is `remoteAddr + \"\\n\" + username` (`:66-68`); 5 failures / 15 min window / 15 min lockout; purge once a minute |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java` | Cookie read/write | `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax`, `Path=<contextPath>`, **`Secure` only when `request.isSecure()`** (`:51-63`); validates the incoming id against a strict shape before any map lookup |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Responses.java` | Response writers | `no-store` on every auth page; JSON gets `nosniff`; **HTML gets no `X-Content-Type-Options`, `X-Frame-Options`, CSP, HSTS or `Referrer-Policy`** (`:17-33`) |\n| `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Html.java`, `Json.java` | Output encoding | Escapes `& < > \" '` for HTML/text and attributes; JSON escapes quotes and control characters |\n| `tools/tomcat-env.sh` | Provisions a throwaway Tomcat and its `server.xml` | Generates a 2-day self-signed cert with the **hardcoded password `changeit`** (`:59-62`); cleartext keystore password in the generated file (`:83`); plain HTTP connector with only `redirectPort` (`:78-79`); access log records `%r` |\n| `run-demo.sh` | Scripted `curl` walkthrough | Sends the **password as a command-line argument** (`:73`), prints it (`:91`), writes the cookie jar and raw `Set-Cookie` dumps under `build/` (`:24`, `:52`, `:61`) |\n| `build.sh` | `javac` + `jar` | Compiles with `-Xlint:all -Werror`; no secrets; not security-relevant beyond that |\n| `tests/java/UnitChecks.java`, `tests/unit-checks.sh`, `tests/end-to-end.sh` | Test harnesses | Assert single-use tokens, forged-id rejection, timing equalisation, lockout scoping; **also assert the cleartext-cookie behaviour as intended**, and carry the plaintext passwords |\n| `README.md` | Documentation | Repeats both demo passwords; states that real TLS, a real account store, shared session storage, Argon2id, edge rate limiting, CSP and an audit trail are still needed |\n\n## 3. Skills Applied\n\n- `review-code-for-security-vulnerabilities`: router — read every source file first to establish what the code actually does, then selected the specialised skills below by matching that behaviour against the skill library.\n- `performing-cryptographic-audit-of-application`: applied to `PasswordService.java` and `SessionStore.java` — KDF choice and iteration bounds, salt/hash lengths, CSPRNG usage, constant-time comparison, and the \"Hardcoded Secrets — keys/passwords in source code\" class that covers `web.xml:60-68` and `tools/tomcat-env.sh:59-62`.\n- `implementing-secret-scanning-with-gitleaks`: applied to the repository as a credential store — the seed passwords, the `changeit` keystore password, and the cookie jars written under `build/` are exactly the patterns a `gitleaks detect --source .` gate is meant to fail on.\n- `testing-api-authentication-weaknesses`: applied to `LoginServlet`/`LogoutServlet`/`SessionStore` — token validation and lifecycle, session fixation, enumeration resistance, and Step 6's password-policy and enumeration-by-response-difference checks.\n- `testing-for-broken-access-control`: applied to `AuthFilter`/`AccountServlet`/`StatusServlet` — default-deny mapping, path derivation (`pathOf`), open-path inventory, and the horizontal/vertical privilege matrix for the four endpoints.\n- `performing-security-headers-audit`: applied to `Responses.java` and the generated `server.xml` — HSTS, HTTP→HTTPS redirect, `X-Frame-Options`/`frame-ancestors`, `X-Content-Type-Options`, `Referrer-Policy`, cache directives, and the cookie-attribute audit.\n- `configuring-tls-1-3-for-secure-communications`: applied to `tools/tomcat-env.sh`'s connector and certificate setup — certificate trust, key exchange, and the step that mandates an HSTS header alongside the TLS listener.\n- `implementing-api-rate-limiting-and-throttling`: applied to `LoginThrottle.java` and `LoginServlet.doPost` — the distinction between a per-account *failure* limit and a per-IP *request* limit, global limits, and the rule that rate limiting must not be the sole defence.\n- `performing-csrf-attack-simulation`: applied to `LoginServlet`, `LogoutServlet` and the `AccountServlet` sign-out form — state-changing inventory, single-use token binding, cross-user token reuse, and `SameSite`/Origin validation as the remaining defence on `/logout`.\n- `testing-for-xss-vulnerabilities`: applied to `Html.java`, `Json.java`, `Responses.java` and `AuthFilter.refuse` — output-context cap username length before keying; derive the key from a normalised identifier.\n- Cap concurrency: reject with 429 + Retry-After when the KDF pool is saturated; run the stretch on a bounded pool so it cannot consume the whole 32-thread executor.\n- Prefer an edge rate limit; move the stretch off the request-critical path or use an honest cost knob.\nCode:\n```java\n        // Cost is charged per *request*, not per recorded failure, and the budget is bounded.\n        if (!context.loginBudget().tryAcquire(request.getRemoteAddr())) {\n            Responses.html(..., SC_TOO_MANY_REQUESTS, ...)\n```\nAnd throttle bounded:\n```java\n    private final Map<String, Attempt> attempts = new BoundedMap<>(MAX_KEYS, window.plus(lockout));\n```\nI'll write something concrete but short.\n\n### SEC-04: Missing response headers, no HSTS\nLocation: `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Responses.java:17-33`.\nFix: add a `securityHeaders(response, secure)` that sets X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff, X-Frame-Options: DENY, Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'; frame-ancestors 'none'; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'self'; connect-src 'self'; img-src 'self'; script-src 'none'; Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin; Permissions-Policy; Mention it.\n\n### SEC-05: Log forging via username\nLocation: `LoginServlet.java:112-115` (also `:80`).\nVulnerable code: \n```java\n            if (!credentialsOk) {\n                context.throttle().recordFailure(throttleKey, Instant.now());\n                LOG.log(Level.FINE, \"Rejected sign-in for user ''{0}'' from {1}\",\n                        new Object[] {username, request.getRemoteAddr()});\n```\nFix: log the validated identifier only, with a length cap and CR/LF strip:\n```java\n                LOG.log(Level.FINE, \"Rejected sign-in for user ''{0}'' from {1}\",\n                        new Object[] {safeForLog(username), request.getRemoteAddr()});\n...\n    /** Echoes back only what the username whitelist allows, so a log line cannot smuggle a second entry. */\n    private static String safeForLog(String username) {\n        return USERNAME.matcher(username).matches() ? username : \"<rejected:\" + username.length() + '>';\n    }\n```\nGood — reuses the existing USERNAME pattern. Note `request.getRemoteAddr()` is safe (container-parsed, not header-derived).\n\n### SEC-06: lockout design\nLocation: `LoginThrottle.java:66-68`.\nVulnerable:\n```java\n    public static String keyFor(String remoteAddr, String username) {\n        return remoteAddr + \"\\n\" + username;\n    }\n```\nWhy: 5 failures per (IP, username) → attacker with one IP and a list of 10 000 usernames gets 50 000 guesses with no lockout; the account-level limit that exists is per-IP too, so no global per-account cap → password spray of one popular account from many IPs is unlimited. Conversely a single shared source IP (NAT, VPN, office) can be locked out of any account by an attacker who burns 5 failures — denial of service against a named user, and the map never distinguishes provenance.\nFix: split the two axes: per-account *global* failure budget (small, with backoff) + per-IP budget; and per-IP *request* rate; and don't lock a victim out of an account because of another client (prefer CAPTCHA/step-up over hard lockout).\nCode sketch:\n```java\n    /** One axis: the caller. */\n    public static String keyForAddress(String remoteAddr) { return \"ip:\" + remoteAddr; }\n    /** The other axis: the account, counted across all callers. */\n    public static String keyForAccount(String username) { return \"acct:\" + username.toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT); }\n```\nand in the servlet check both verdicts, then record against both.\n\n### SEC-07: PBKDF2 min bound 1,000\nLocation: `PasswordService.java:25-26`.\nVulnerable:\n```java\n    static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000;\n    static final int MAX_ITERATIONS = 5_000_000;\n```\nWhy: the class validates stored records against this floor, so a record with `pbkdf2-sha256$1000$...` is accepted and verified — verification then costs ~1000× less than the deployment's 240 000, so a database or config-substituted weak record silently lowers the bar; there's no rehash-on-login and no warning. OWASP 800-63B says PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 ≥ 600 000 (NSM 2024: 600000) (demo at 240 000 is already below). Also `MIN_ITERATIONS` is what `chargeRefusedAttempt` and `verify` will happily stretch at, so an attacker who can influence a stored record gets a cheap oracle.\nFix:\n```java\n    /** Floor: below this a stretched password is not worth verifying; rehash instead of accepting. */\n    static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 210_000;\n    /** Current target, raised with hardware. Anything below it is rehashed on next successful login. */\n    static final int TARGET_ITERATIONS = 600_000;\n```\nplus reject-on-parse + rehash-on-login:\n```java\n        if (iters < MIN_ITERATIONS) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"stored password has \" + iters\n                    + \" iterations, below the floor of \" + MIN_ITERATIONS + \"; re-hash it\");\n        }\n```\nand in LoginServlet after successful verify: `if (stored.iterations() < context.passwords().iterations()) { context.users().replace(username, context.passwords().hash(password)); }` (needs a store method — mention).\n\n### SEC-08: hardcoded keystore password + untrusted cert\nLocation: `tools/tomcat-env.sh:59-62`.\nVulnerable:\n```sh\n  keytool -genkeypair -alias demo -keyalg RSA -keysize 2048 \\\n          -dname \"CN=localhost, O=throwaway demo, C=US\" \\\n          -validity 2 -storepass changeit -keypass changeit \\\n          -keystore \"$keystore\" -storetype PKCS12 >/dev/null 2>&1\n```\nAlso server.xml contains `certificateKeystorePassword=\"changeit\"` at line 83.\nWhy: key at rest protected by a published password in a committed script; anyone who reads demo.jks (world-readable under CATALINA_BASE, no chmod 600) can decrypt the private key, and the key is also in shell history / ps. Combined with a self-signed 2-day cert, the only thing teaching clients is `-k` — which is exactly how the test suite authenticates TLS (`tests/end-to-end.sh:149`), training everyone to ignore certificate validation, and the private key then can't be distinguished from an attacker's if reused.\nFix: generate a random per-run password, pass it via env (keytool supports `-storepass:env`), protect the file, and never write the secret into a committed file:\n```sh\n  local keystore=\"$CATALINA_BASE/demo.jks\"\n  local storepass_file=\"$CATALINA_BASE/demo.pw\"\n  umask 077\n  openssl rand -base64 24 > \"$storepass_file\"          # random per run, never committed\n  DEMO_STOREPASS=\"$(cat \"$storepass_file\")\" keytool -genkeypair -alias demo -keyalg RSA -keysize 2048 \\\n      -dname \"CN=localhost, O=throwaway demo, C=US\" \\\n      -validity 2 -storepass:env DEMO_STOREPASS -keypass:env DEMO_STOREPASS \\\n      -keystore \"$keystore\" -storetype PKCS12 >/dev/null 2>&1\n```\nand in server.xml reference a placeholder that the launcher substitutes, or use `-Ddemo.storepass=`. Keep simple.\n\n### SEC-09: session ids on disk + password in argv/transcript\nLocation: `run-demo.sh:24` (also 52-68, 72-74, 91).\nVulnerable:\n```bash\nJAR=\"$BUILD_DIR/demo-cookies.txt\"\n...\ncurl -sS -o \"$BUILD_DIR/demo-login.html\" -D \"$BUILD_DIR/demo-login.hdr\" -c \"$JAR\" \"$BASE_URL/login\"\n...\nshow \"cookie jar now holds: $(sed 's/^#HttpOnly_//' \"$JAR\" | awk '!/^#/ && NF >= 7 {print $6 \"=\" substr($7,1,12) \"...\"}')\"\n```\nWhy: the jar (including the HttpOnly-marked session id) is written under the world-readable build dir and left behind after the run — build.sh cleans $BUILD_DIR, run-demo.sh does not, so a valid admin session id persists on disk for anyone who can read the tree; `curl`'s argv exposes the real password to any local process via `ps`/proc; the transcript prints the password in plaintext twice.\nFix: keep the jar in a private temp dir with a trap, and pass the secret over a channel that isn't argv:\n```bash\nWORK=$(mktemp -d \"${TMPDIR:-/tmp}/auth-demo.XXXXXX\"); chmod 700 \"$WORK\"; trap 'rm -rf \"$WORK\"' EXIT\nJAR=\"$WORK/cookies.txt\"\n...\n    --data-urlencode \"password@-$WORK/password.txt\" \\\n```\nHmm `--data-urlencode name@file` reads from file. Use:\n```bash\nprintf '%s' \"$DEMO_PASSWORD\" > \"$WORK/password.txt\"; chmod 600 \"$WORK/password.txt\"\n    --data-urlencode \"password@$WORK/password.txt\" \\\n```\nGood, plus remove the credential from the printed line at :91.\n\n### SEC-10: /status information disclosure\nLocation: `StatusServlet.java:36-49`.\nVulnerable: excerpt of :36-49 (trimmed).\nWhy: unauthenticated endpoint reveals the KDF algorithm and exact iteration count (lets an attacker price an offline crack before stealing anything), the cookie name and attribute set (confirms that capturing `app_session` is the win, and whether it's HttpOnly/Secure), and the lockout parameters — `loginMaxFailures`/`window`/`lockout` tell a sprayer exactly how many guesses per username per window are free (see SEC-03/SEC-06), and `sessionIdleTimeoutSeconds` bounds the replay window. The endpoint's stated purpose (a demo's self-description) doesn't require any of it to be public.\nFix: keep only what's needed pre-auth:\n```java\n        Responses.json(response, HttpServletResponse.SC_OK, Json.object(\n                Json.field(\"application\", \"session-cookie-auth-demo\"),\n                Json.field(\"signedIn\", signedIn)));\n```\nand expose the policy fields only to a signed-in admin (`if (!context.isAdmin(principal)) ...`), or move them behind `AuthFilter` at a new `/admin/config`.\n\n### SEC-11: derived key not zeroed\nLocation: `PasswordService.java:137-140` (+ `:142-153`).\nVulnerable:\n```java\n    public void chargeRefusedAttempt(char[] password) {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(password, \"password\");\n        derive(password, timingReference.salt(), timingReference.iterations());\n    }\n```\nWhy: `derive` returns a freshly allocated 32-byte key that `verify` zeroes in its `finally` (`:118-125`) but this path drops without wiping; on this path the derived value is garbage (wrong salt) yet it is still a `char[]`-derived secret sitting in a heap array that a later heap dump/core file/crash dump hands over.\nFix:\n```java\n    public void chargeRefusedAttempt(char[] password) {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(password, \"password\");\n        byte[] digest = derive(password, timingReference.salt(), timingReference.iterations());\n        try {\n            // Nothing is compared here; the work is the point, and the scratch key is wiped like\n            // every other derived key.\n        } finally {\n            if (digest != null) {\n                Arrays.fill(digest, (byte) 0);\n            }\n        }\n    }\n```\nGood.\n\n§6 Verified controls — bullets:\n- Session id entropy/origin (SessionStore.java:30-31, :39, :66-67, :219) — 32 bytes SecureRandom, Base64url no padding, never derived from anything guessable; `SESSION_ID` shape check before any store lookup (SessionServlets.java:23-25) so junk is rejected rather than looked up.\n- Session fixation closed (LoginServlet.java:125-127) — form record invalidated before the authenticated id is minted, and e2e asserts the post-login id differs from the pre-login one (end-to-end.sh:109-110).\n- Constant-time comparison of every secret (PasswordService.java:120, SessionStore.java:89-90, :135, SessionServlets.java:78) — `MessageDigest.isEqual` for digest, session id, CSRF token, salt; Detailed Findings\n\n### SEC-01: Plaintext administrator password hardcoded in the deployment descriptor\n\n**Severity:** High\n**CWE:** CWE-798: Use of Hard-coded Credentials\n**Location:** `src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:60-68`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```xml\n<!-- src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:60-68 -->\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.alice</param-name>\n    <param-value>Alice!2345 | admin,user</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.bob</param-name>\n    <param-value>B0b-the-Builder | user</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n```\n\n`AuthContext` reads these verbatim and turns them into live accounts at startup — `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java:99-102`:\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java:99-102\n            String[] spec = String.valueOf(context.getInitParameter(name)).split(\"\\\\|\", -1);\n            String password = spec[0].trim();\n            Set<String> roles = spec.length > 1 ? splitRoles(spec[1]) : Set.of(\"user\");\n            seed.put(username, new SeedAccount(password.toCharArray(), roles));\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `Alice!2345` is not a sample hash or a placeholder — it is the credential that grants the `admin` role, and passwords are the only authentication factor this application has. Anyone who can read the repository, a clone, a fork, a CI artifact, a backup, or the packaged WAR (the same credential is the deployment configuration; no separate secret ever exists) can sign in over the network. `src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:57-58` asserts \"These are throwaway credentials for a demo on localhost\", which is exactly the reasoning hard-coded credentials survive on: the value is copied into every derived environment, is embedded in every WAR that `build.sh` produces, is re-typed into `run-demo.sh:73` and printed in the demo transcript at `run-demo.sh:91`, is carried in curl's argv in `tests/end-to-end.sh` (visible to any local process through `ps`/`/proc`), and stays in git history after the line is deleted. It is also unrotatable without a rebuild, identical across every install of this artifact, and invisible to any secret-management or rotation process. A gitleaks-style pre-commit scan of this repository flags it immediately.\n\n**Fix:** Let the descriptor name the *source* of the secret and have the application refuse to start when the source is empty — never fall back to a literal:\n\n```xml\n<!-- src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml -->\n  <!-- Value is \"NAME_OF_ENV_VAR_WITH_PASSWORD | role,role\". The descriptor names where the\n       secret lives; it never contains one. -->\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.alice</param-name>\n    <param-value>AUTH_SEED_ALICE | admin,user</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java (seedUsers)\n            String[] spec = String.valueOf(context.getInitParameter(name)).split(\"\\\\|\", -1);\n            String secretSource = spec[0].trim();\n            Set<String> roles = spec.length > 1 ? splitRoles(spec[1]) : Set.of(\"user\");\n            String secret = System.getenv(secretSource);   // or a mounted secret file\n            if (secret == null || secret.isBlank()) {\n                // No literal default: a missing secret is a deployment error, not a default password.\n                throw new IllegalStateException(\"seed account '\" + username + \"': \" + secretSource\n                        + \" is not set; refusing to start with a missing credential\");\n            }\n            seed.put(username, new SeedAccount(secret.toCharArray(), roles));\n```\n\nThen rotate `Alice!2345` and `B0b-the-Builder` (they are burned the moment they are committed), purge them from git history (`git filter-repo --replace-text`), rewrite `run-demo.sh` and `tests/end-to-end.sh` to read them from the environment (see SEC-09), and add the scan gate to `build.sh`:\n\n```bash\n# build.sh - fail the build on any secret-shaped value in tracked files\ngitleaks detect --source . --redact || { echo \"secret scan failed\" >&2; exit 1; }\n```\n\n### SEC-02: Session cookie transmitted in cleartext: TLS is never enforced and HSTS is absent\n\n**Severity:** High\n**CWE:** CWE-319: Cleartext Transmission of Sensitive Information\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java:51-63`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java:51-63\n    static void writeSessionId(HttpServletResponse response, String sessionId, Duration maxAge,\n                               boolean secure, String path) {\n        Cookie cookie = new Cookie(AuthContext.COOKIE_NAME, sessionId);\n        cookie.setPath(path);\n        cookie.setHttpOnly(true);\n        cookie.setSecure(secure);\n        cookie.setAttribute(\"SameSite\", \"Lax\");\n        if (maxAge != null) {\n            long seconds = Math.max(maxAge.getSeconds(), 0L);\n            cookie.setMaxAge(seconds > Integer.MAX_VALUE ? Integer.MAX_VALUE : (int) seconds);\n        }\n        response.addCookie(cookie);\n    }\n```\n\nEvery caller passes the *request's* own protocol as `secure` — `LoginServlet.java:137`, `LoginServlet.java:153`, `AccountServlet.java:52-53`, `LogoutServlet.java:34` all pass `request.isSecure()`. Nothing in the descriptor forces TLS, and `src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:75-81` configures only `http-only`:\n\n```xml\n<!-- src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:75-81 -->\n  <session-config>\n    <session-timeout>30</session-timeout>\n    <cookie-config>\n      <http-only>true</http-only>\n    </cookie-config>\n    <tracking-mode>COOKIE</tracking-mode>\n  </session-config>\n```\n\nand the launcher serves the application on a plain HTTP connector:\n\n```xml\n<!-- tools/tomcat-env.sh:78-79 -->\n    <Connector port=\"$HTTP_PORT\" protocol=\"HTTP/1.1\" executor=\"demo\"\n               connectionTimeout=\"20000\" redirectPort=\"$HTTPS_PORT\"/>\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** The whole security model rests on one property: the 256-bit value in `app_session` is unguessable, so whoever possesses it *is* the signed-in user, including the admin. Over the HTTP connector the credential — the cookie header on every request, and the `Set-Cookie` header at login — travels in clear text, so anyone on the path (a hostile or merely misconfigured access point, a compromised router, ARP position on the local segment, a mirror port) recovers it with a packet capture and needs no further attack. `redirectPort` is not enforcement: it applies only to requests the container itself classifies as confidential, and with no `<transport-guarantee>` anywhere in `web.xml` no request is so classified, so nothing ever redirects. Because the flag is derived from `request.isSecure()`, the first HTTP request to a fresh browser sets a cookie without `Secure`; that cookie is then replayed onto the HTTPS connector too, so the session is disclosed even for a user who later reaches the encrypted port, and there is no HSTS to make the downgrade non-silent. The behaviour is not incidental, either: `tests/end-to-end.sh:125-128` asserts it as expected (\"over plain HTTP the cookie is deliberately not marked Secure\"), and `tools/tomcat-env.sh:53-57` states that HTTP on `BASE_URL` is deliberate. That is the intended shape of a demo, but a reviewer must report it as the reportable defect: any deployment that inherits this launcher, or any real use of it, ships credentials in clear text. The HTTPS connector that does exist has no HSTS either, and its certificate is a two-day self-signed throwaway (SEC-08), which teaches every consumer to pass `-k` (`tests/end-to-end.sh:149`).\n\n**Fix:** Enforce confidentiality in the descriptor, stop trusting the request's own scheme for the cookie flag, and emit HSTS:\n\n```xml\n<!-- src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:75-81 -->\n  <session-config>\n    <session-timeout>30</session-timeout>\n    <cookie-config>\n      <http-only>true</http-only>\n      <secure>true</secure>\n    </cookie-config>\n    <tracking-mode>COOKIE</tracking-mode>\n  </session-config>\n\n  <!-- The container then refuses to serve the conf"}}],"stop_reason":"tool_use","usage":{"input_tokens":157493,"output_tokens":2497,"cache_read_input_tokens":0,"total_tokens":159990}}},{"type":"user","uuid":"7468797d-490f-40c0-bee1-04e61e0ee216","session_id":"45c8944e-d3f6-41a6-ac2d-c70f0846fd52","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_09e3894034b646ff917b0e8c","is_error":true,"content":"File /data/results-cyber/qwen/set_a/sallie/1/basic/security_review.md has not been read in this session.
