Detailed Findings\n\n### SEC-01: Plaintext administrator password hardcoded in the deployment descriptor\n\n**Severity:** High\n\n**CWE:** CWE-798: Use of Hard-coded Credentials\n\n**Location:** `src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:60-68`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```xml\n// src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:60-68\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.alice</param-name>\n    <param-value>Alice!2345 | admin,user</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.bob</param-name>\n    <param-value>B0b-the-Builder | user</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n```\n\n`AuthContext.seedUsers` consumes these verbatim — the literal *is* the credential, hashed only after it has been read out of the descriptor (`src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java:99-102`):\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java:99-102\n            String[] spec = String.valueOf(context.getInitParameter(name)).split(\"\\\\|\", -1);\n            String password = spec[0].trim();\n            Set<String> roles = spec.length > 1 ? splitRoles(spec[1]) : Set.of(\"user\");\n            seed.put(username, new SeedAccount(password.toCharArray(), roles));\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `Alice!2345` is not a placeholder — it satisfies the project's own candidate rules and grants the `admin` role, and it is committed in a file that is packaged into `session-cookie-auth-demo.war`. Everything that can read the artifact therefore holds an administrator credential: anyone with read access to the repository or its git history (the value survives any later edit), anyone who can pull a container image or a build artifact, anyone who can read a CI log or a stack trace that echoes init parameters. The same literal is repeated at `run-demo.sh:73`, `run-demo.sh:91` and `tests/end-to-end.sh:44-45`, so it also lands in shell history, process listings and CI output. Because the credential is compiled into the deployment, rotating it means rebuilding and redeploying the WAR, which in practice means it never rotates. This is the classic hard-coded-credential failure: the secret cannot be changed per environment, cannot be scoped to the principals that need it, and cannot be revoked. The comment at `web.xml:57-58` (\"throwaway credentials for a demo on localhost\") describes intent, not risk — this repository's README presents the code as a pattern to copy, and a copied descriptor carries the credential with it.\n\n**Fix:** Keep the descriptor as *policy* and move the credential to a secret store, so the file that is committed names a source rather than a value. Refuse to start when the secret is missing instead of falling back to a default — a fail-open default is how this class of bug survives its own fix.\n\n```xml\n<!-- src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml (corrected) -->\n  <!-- Names where the credential comes from; contains no secret. -->\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.alice.source</param-name>\n    <param-value>SEED_PASSWORD_ALICE</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.alice.roles</param-name>\n    <param-value>admin,user</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java (corrected)\n        for (String username : seedUsernames) {\n            String source = String.valueOf(\n                    context.getInitParameter(\"seed.user.\" + username + \".source\"));\n            String password = System.getenv(source);          // or the platform secret manager SDK\n            if (password == null || password.isBlank()) {\n                // No literal default: a missing secret must stop startup, not fall back.\n                throw new IllegalStateException(\n                        \"Seed account '\" + username + \"' has no credential in \" + source);\n            }\n            Set<String> roles = splitRoles(\n                    context.getInitParameter(\"seed.user.\" + username + \".roles\"));\n            seed.put(username, new SeedAccount(password.toCharArray(), roles));\n        }\n```\n\nThen: rotate both passwords (they must be assumed compromised — they are in history), delete them from `run-demo.sh` and `tests/end-to-end.sh` and read them from the environment there too, rewrite history with `git filter-repo --replace-text` if the repository was ever shared, and add a gate so this cannot recur:\n\n```bash\n# build.sh (append)\ngitleaks detect --source . --redact || { echo \"secrets committed\"; exit 1; }\n```\n\n### SEC-02: Session cookie transmitted in cleartext: TLS is never enforced and HSTS is absent\n\n**Severity:** High\n\n**CWE:** CWE-319: Authentication of a Security-Critical Channel with Passing of Sensitive Information in Cleartext\n\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java:51-63`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java:51-63\n    static void writeSessionId(HttpServletResponse response, String sessionId, Duration maxAge,\n                               boolean secure, String path) {\n        Cookie cookie = new Cookie(AuthContext.COOKIE_NAME, sessionId);\n        cookie.setPath(path);\n        cookie.setHttpOnly(true);\n        cookie.setSecure(secure);\n        cookie.setAttribute(\"SameSite\", \"Lax\");\n        if (maxAge != null) {\n            long seconds = Math.max(maxAge.getSeconds(), 0L);\n            cookie.setMaxAge(seconds > Integer.MAX_VALUE ? Integer.MAX_VALUE : (int) seconds);\n        }\n        response.addCookie(cookie);\n    }\n```\n\nEvery caller passes the scheme of the *request that happens to be answering*, so a session minted over HTTP is issued without `Secure`: `LoginServlet.java:137-139` and `:153`, `AccountServlet.java:49-50`, `LogoutServlet.java:34`. The descriptor does not compensate — there is no `<secure>` element and no `<transport-guarantee>` anywhere in the session config (`src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:75-81`):\n\n```xml\n// src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:75-81\n  <session-config>\n    <session-timeout>30</session-timeout>\n    <cookie-config>\n      <http-only>true</http-only>\n    </cookie-config>\n    <tracking-mode>COOKIE</tracking-mode>\n  </session-config>\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `Secure` is the only cookie attribute that keeps a session id out of a cleartext request, and here it is derived from the transport an attacker can influence rather than from policy. Because nothing marks the application's URLs confidential, the container never redirects: `redirectPort` in the generated `server.xml` (`tools/tomcat-env.sh:78-79`) only takes effect for traffic the Servlet spec classifies as transport-protected, and with no `<transport-guarantee>` the plain HTTP connector simply serves the app. That is the path the demo actually exercises — `run-demo.sh:20` uses `BASE_URL` over HTTP, and `tests/end-to-end.sh:98-101` asserts the cookie is *not* marked Secure as the expected outcome, so the weakness is currently load-bearing in the test suite. The consequence is total: any on-path party on the shared network (public Wi-Fi, a compromised router, ARP/TLS-stripping position) reads `app_session=<id>` from `GET /account` and replays it verbatim — full session hijack, up to the `admin` account from SEC-01, with no password involved and the login throttle irrelevant. The absence of HSTS removes the one mechanism that would stop a downgrade from HTTPS to HTTP: there is no `Strict-Transport-Security` header in the application (`Responses.java:17-33` emits only cache headers) and none in the connector configuration (`tools/tomcat-env.sh:80-86`), so a single stripped request is enough. Note the session id is otherwise well built (256-bit, `SecureRandom`, strict shape validation on read) — its strength does not survive sending it unencrypted.\n\n**Fix:** Decide the policy in configuration and make the code refuse to violate it. Mark the cookie Secure unconditionally, keep the container in charge of the redirect, and add HSTS:\n\n```xml\n<!-- src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml (corrected) -->\n  <session-config>\n    <session-timeout>30</session-timeout>\n    <cookie-config>\n      <http-only>true</http-only>\n      <secure>true</secure>\n    </cookie-config>\n    <tracking-mode>COOKIE</tracking-mode>\n  </session-config>\n\n  <!-- Make the container own the redirect instead of relying on a browser being lucky. -->\n  <security-constraint>\n    <web-resource-collection>\n      <web-resource-name>everything</web-resource-name>\n      <url-pattern>/*</url-pattern>\n    </web-resource-collection>\n    <user-data-constraint>\n      <transport-guarantee>CONFIDENTIAL</transport-guarantee>\n    </user-data-constraint>\n  </security-constraint>\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java (corrected)\n    static void writeSessionId(HttpServletResponse response, String sessionId, Duration maxAge,\n                              String path) {\n        Cookie cookie = new Cookie(AuthContext.COOKIE_NAME, sessionId);\n        cookie.setPath(path);\n        cookie.setHttpOnly(true);\n        // Policy, not observation: never mint a cookie that TLS stripping could carry.\n        cookie.setSecure(true);\n        cookie.setAttribute(\"SameSite\", \"Lax\");\n        if (maxAge != null) {\n            long seconds = Math.max(maxAge.getSeconds(), 0L);\n            cookie.setMaxAge(seconds > Integer.MAX_VALUE ? Integer.MAX_VALUE : (int) seconds);\n        }\n        response.addCookie(cookie);\n    }\n```\n\nDrop the `secure` parameter from all four call sites (and the assertion at `tests/end-to-end.sh:98-101`) so the insecure path is not merely unused but impossible. Detailed Findings\n\n### SEC-01: Plaintext administrator password hardcoded in the deployment descriptor\n\n**Severity:** High\n\n**CWE:** CWE-798: Use of Hard-coded Credentials\n\n**Location:** `src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:60-68`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```xml\n// src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:60-68\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.alice</param-name>\n    <param-value>Alice!2345 | admin,user</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.bob</param-name>\n    <param-value>B0b-the-Builder | user</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n```\n\n`AuthContext.seedUsers` consumes these verbatim — the literal *is* the credential; it is hashed only after being read out of the descriptor (`src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java:99-102`):\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java:99-102\n            String[] spec = String.valueOf(context.getInitParameter(name)).split(\"\\\\|\", -1);\n            String password = spec[0].trim();\n            Set<String> roles = spec.length > 1 ? splitRoles(spec[1]) : Set.of(\"user\");\n            seed.put(username, new SeedAccount(password.toCharArray(), roles));\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `Alice!2345` is not a placeholder — it satisfies the project's own candidate rules and carries the `admin` role, and it is committed in a file that is packaged into `session-cookie-auth-demo.war`. Everything that can read the artifact therefore holds an administrator credential: anyone with read access to the repository or its git history (the value survives any later edit), anyone who can pull a build artifact or container image, anyone who can read CI output or a startup failure that echoes init parameters. The same literal is repeated at `run-demo.sh:73` and `run-demo.sh:91` and in `tests/end-to-end.sh`, so it also reaches shell history, process listings and test logs. Because the credential is compiled into the deployment, rotating it means rebuilding and redeploying the WAR — in practice it never rotates, and it cannot differ per environment. That is the whole of the hard-coded-credential failure mode: unrotatable, unscoped, unrevocable. The comment at `web.xml:57-58` (\"throwaway credentials for a demo on localhost\") states intent, not risk; the README presents this code as a pattern to copy, and a copied descriptor carries the credential with it.\n\n**Fix:** Keep the descriptor as *policy* and move the secret out, so the committed file names a source rather than a value. Refuse to start when the secret is absent — a fail-open default is how this bug survives its own fix.\n\n```xml\n<!-- src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml (corrected) -->\n  <!-- Names where the credential comes from; contains no secret. -->\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.alice.source</param-name>\n    <param-value>SEED_PASSWORD_ALICE</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n  <context-param>\n    <param-name>seed.user.alice.roles</param-name>\n    <param-value>admin,user</param-value>\n  </context-param>\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/AuthContext.java (corrected)\n        for (String username : seedUsernames) {\n            String source = String.valueOf(\n                    context.getInitParameter(\"seed.user.\" + username + \".source\"));\n            String password = System.getenv(source);   // or the platform secret-manager SDK\n            if (password == null || password.isBlank()) {\n                // No literal default: a missing secret stops startup, it does not fall back.\n                throw new IllegalStateException(\n                        \"Seed account '\" + username + \"' has no credential in \" + source);\n            }\n            Set<String> roles = splitRoles(\n                    context.getInitParameter(\"seed.user.\" + username + \".roles\"));\n            seed.put(username, new SeedAccount(password.toCharArray(), roles));\n        }\n```\n\nThen: rotate both passwords (assume them compromised), replace the literals in `run-demo.sh` and `tests/end-to-end.sh` with environment lookups, rewrite history with `git filter-repo --replace-text` if the repository was ever shared, and add a gate so it cannot recur:\n\n```bash\n# build.sh (append)\ngitleaks detect --source . --redact || { echo \"secrets committed\"; exit 1; }\n```\n\n### SEC-02: Session cookie transmitted in cleartext: TLS is never enforced and HSTS is absent\n\n**Severity:** High\n\n**CWE:** CWE-319: Authentication of a Security-Critical Channel with Passing of Sensitive Information in Cleartext\n\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java:51-63`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java:51-63\n    static void writeSessionId(HttpServletResponse response, String sessionId, Duration maxAge,\n                               boolean secure, String path) {\n        Cookie cookie = new Cookie(AuthContext.COOKIE_NAME, sessionId);\n        cookie.setPath(path);\n        cookie.setHttpOnly(true);\n        cookie.setSecure(secure);\n        cookie.setAttribute(\"SameSite\", \"Lax\");\n        if (maxAge != null) {\n            long seconds = Math.max(maxAge.getSeconds(), 0L);\n            cookie.setMaxAge(seconds > Integer.MAX_VALUE ? Integer.MAX_VALUE : (int) seconds);\n        }\n        response.addCookie(cookie);\n    }\n```\n\nEvery caller passes the scheme of whichever request happens to be answering, so a session minted over HTTP is issued without `Secure`: `LoginServlet.java:137-139`, `LoginServlet.java:153`, `AccountServlet.java:49-50`, `LogoutServlet.java:34`. The descriptor does not compensate — no `<secure>` element and no `<transport-guarantee>` anywhere in the session config (`src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:75-81`):\n\n```xml\n// src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml:75-81\n  <session-config>\n    <session-timeout>30</session-timeout>\n    <cookie-config>\n      <http-only>true</http-only>\n    </cookie-config>\n    <tracking-mode>COOKIE</tracking-mode>\n  </session-config>\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `Secure` is the only cookie attribute that keeps a session id out of a cleartext request, and here it is derived from the transport an attacker can influence rather than from policy. Nothing marks the application's URLs confidential, so the container never redirects: `redirectPort` in the generated `server.xml` (`tools/tomcat-env.sh:78-79`) only applies to traffic the Servlet spec classifies as transport-protected, and with no `<transport-guarantee>` the plain HTTP connector simply serves the application. That is the path the demo exercises — `run-demo.sh:20` uses the HTTP `BASE_URL`, and `tests/end-to-end.sh` asserts that \"over plain HTTP the cookie is deliberately not marked Secure\", which makes the weakness load-bearing in the test suite. The consequence is total: any on-path party on a shared network (public Wi-Fi, a compromised router, an ARP or TLS-stripping position) reads `app_session=<id>` from `GET /account` and replays it verbatim — full hijack of any account including the `admin` from SEC-01, with no password involved and the login throttle irrelevant. HSTS, the one mechanism that would stop an HTTPS→HTTP downgrade, appears nowhere: `Responses.java:17-33` emits only cache headers and the connector block (`tools/tomcat-env.sh:80-86`) sets no `StrictHostConfig`/HSTS, so a single stripped request is enough. The session id itself is otherwise well built — 256 bits from `SecureRandom`, strictly shape-validated on read (`Cookies.java:20-21`, `:41`) — but cryptographic strength does not survive transmission in the clear.\n\n**Fix:** Decide the policy in configuration and make the code unable to violate it: mark the cookie Secure unconditionally, let the container own the redirect, and add HSTS.\n\n```xml\n<!-- src/main/webapp/WEB-INF/web.xml (corrected) -->\n  <session-config>\n    <session-timeout>30</session-timeout>\n    <cookie-config>\n      <http-only>true</http-only>\n      <secure>true</secure>\n    </cookie-config>\n    <tracking-mode>COOKIE</tracking-mode>\n  </session-config>\n\n  <!-- The container owns the redirect; nobody has to be lucky enough to type https. -->\n  <security-constraint>\n    <web-resource-collection>\n      <web-resource-name>all</web-resource-name>\n      <url-pattern>/*</url-pattern>\n    </web-resource-collection>\n    <user-data-constraint>\n      <transport-guarantee>CONFIDENTIAL</transport-guarantee>\n    </user-data-constraint>\n  </security-constraint>\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Cookies.java (corrected)\n    static void writeSessionId(HttpServletResponse response, String sessionId, Duration maxAge,\n                               String path) {\n        Cookie cookie = new Cookie(AuthContext.COOKIE_NAME, sessionId);\n        cookie.setPath(path);\n        cookie.setHttpOnly(true);\n        // Policy, not observation: never mint a cookie that a stripping attack could carry.\n        cookie.setSecure(true);\n        cookie.setAttribute(\"SameSite\", \"Lax\");\n        if (maxAge != null) {\n            long seconds = Math.max(maxAge.getSeconds(), 0L);\n            cookie.setMaxAge(seconds > Integer.MAX_VALUE ? Integer.MAX_VALUE : (int) seconds);\n        }\n        response.addCookie(cookie);\n    }\n```\n\nRemove the `secure` parameter from all four call sites and invert the assertion in `tests/end-to-end.sh` so the insecure path is not merely unused but impossible. Behind a TLS-terminating proxy, derive \"was the client's own connection TLS\" from a configured, trusted proxy header rather than any header the client sends, and still emit HSTS at the edge: `Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains`."}}],"stop_reason":"tool_use","usage":{"input_tokens":150459,"output_tokens":2844,"cache_read_input_tokens":0,"total_tokens":153303}}},{"type":"user","uuid":"e7e32579-f211-4d1a-8186-99d3c34fcb30","session_id":"45c8944e-d3f6-41a6-ac2d-c70f0846fd52","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_d98ff87c7d63485fbe026edb","is_error":false,"content":"The file: /data/results-cyber/qwen/set_a/sallie/1/basic/security_review.md has been updated. Behind a TLS-terminating proxy, derive \"was the client's own connection TLS\" from a configured, trusted proxy header rather than any header the client sends, and still emit HSTS at the edge: `Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains`.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"363425f8-3b86-47c2-9e35-cf34575d96f8","session_id":"45c8944e-d3f6-41a6-ac2d-c70f0846fd52","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"363425f8-3b86-47c2-9e35-cf34575d96f8","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"§5 started with SEC-01 and SEC-02. Behind a TLS-terminating proxy, derive \"was the client's own connection TLS\" from a configured, trusted proxy header rather than any header the client sends, and still emit HSTS at the edge: `Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains`.\n\n### SEC-03: Unauthenticated CPU and memory exhaustion: full PBKDF2 cost per request, throttle bypassable by varying the username\n\n**Severity:** High\n\n**CWE:** CWE-400: Uncontrolled Resource Consumption ('Resource Exhaustion')\n\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java:76-108`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java:73-76\n        String username = trimToEmpty(request.getParameter(\"username\"));\n        char[] password = passwordChars(request.getParameter(\"password\"));\n        boolean remember = \"on\".equals(request.getParameter(\"remember\"));\n        String throttleKey = LoginThrottle.keyFor(request.getRemoteAddr(), username);\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java:97-110\n            Optional<PasswordService.StoredPassword> stored = usernameValid(username)\n                    ? context.users().findPassword(username)\n                    : Optional.empty();\n            boolean credentialsOk = false;\n            if (!PasswordService.isUsableCandidate(password)) {\n                // A candidate that cannot be verified unambiguously still costs a full stretch, so\n                // refusing it is not a fast lane through the endpoint.\n                context.passwords().chargeRefusedAttempt(password);\n            } else if (stored.isEmpty()) {\n                // Same here: \"no such user\" must not come back instantly.\n                context.passwords().chargeRefusedAttempt(password);\n            } else {\n                credentialsOk = context.passwords().verify(password, stored.get());\n            }\n```\n\nThe throttle that gates this path is keyed on the pair formed above (`src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java:66-68`), and the stretch itself is 240 000 PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 iterations (`web.xml:17-20` → `AuthContext.java:57`), served by a 32-thread executor (`tools/tomcat-env.sh:77`).\n\n**Why it is a problem:** The equal-cost design at `:101-107` is correct as anti-enumeration engineering — every request pays the same price so timing cannot reveal whether an account exists — but it means `POST /login` is a fixed-cost CPU bomb available to anyone who can open a socket, and the *only* thing in front of it is a limit whose key the attacker chooses. `keyFor(request.getRemoteAddr(), username)` includes an attacker-supplied username that is never validated before the key is built: `trimToEmpty` (`:187-189`) is the only transformation, and the `USERNAME` pattern at `:36` is consulted later, at `:97`/`:184`, only to decide whether to look the account up. So `alice`, `alice.`, `alice_`, `alice-`, `a` + 63 arbitrary characters each get their **own** five-failure budget, and the failure budget never fills. A few hundred concurrent connections with distinct usernames pin all 32 executor threads in stretching and the site stops serving anyone — permanent self-DoS at trivial client cost, from a single IP, with zero failures recorded. Two amplifiers compound it: the key length is unbounded, so each map entry's *size* is attacker-chosen (a 2 KiB username is accepted here even though `MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS = 1_024` bounds the password at `:38`); and every `GET /login` mints a fresh `SessionStore` record plus a CSRF token and a `Set-Cookie` (`:148-155`), a second unauthenticated allocation path with no limit at all. The throttle map is bounded in *lifetime* — `purgeStale` (`:122-132`) drops entries older than window + lockout, roughly 30 minutes, at most once a minute — so this is exhaustion by cardinality and key size inside that window, not an unbounded leak; that is still enough, because the CPU cost, not the memory, is what takes the site down first. `recordFailure` is already `compute`-atomic (`:94-100`), so none of this is a counter race; it is a missing limit in front of an intentionally expensive operation.\n\n**Fix:** Limit *requests*, not failures, and key the limit on something the attacker does not control. Per-account failure counting stays (it is what stops password guessing), but it can never be the throttle that protects CPU:\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java (corrected)\n        String username = trimToEmpty(request.getParameter(\"username\"));\n        // Normalise and bound BEFORE the value is used as a cache/throttle key or logged.\n        String subject = LoginThrottle.normaliseUsername(username);   // lowercase, <= 64, else \"\"\n        String throttleKey = LoginThrottle.keyFor(callerAddress(request), subject);\n\n        // Cost guard, independent of the failure budget: no attacker-chosen string can\n        // mint an unlimited number of full-cost stretching operations.\n        if (!context.limiter().allowLoginAttempt(callerAddress(request))) {\n            Responses.doNotCache(response);\n            response.setIntHeader(\"Retry-After\", LoginThrottle.RETRY_AFTER_SECONDS);\n            Responses.html(response, HttpServletResponse.SC_TOO_MANY_REQUESTS,\n                    formHtml(GENERIC_FAILURE, \"\", false, token, prefix(request)));\n            return;\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java (corrected)\n    /** Fixed-length, normalised identity: `Alice.` and `alice_` must not mint separate budgets. */\n    public static String normaliseUsername(String username) {\n        if (username == null || !USERNAME.matcher(username).matches()) {\n            return \"\";\n        }\n        return username.toLowerCase(java.util.Locale.ROOT);\n    }\n\n    /** Two limits: a per-caller request rate and a global ceiling that protects the KDF pool. */\n    public boolean allowLoginAttempt(String callerAddress) {\n        Instant now = Instant.now();\n        boolean perCaller = tryAcquire(callerKey(callerAddress), LOGIN_ATTEMPTS_PER_MINUTE, now);\n        boolean global = globalAdmissions.tryAcquire(now);\n        return perCaller && global;   // either exhausted => refuse before any stretching happens\n    }\n```\n\nAlso: bound the throttle map's cardinality (fixed-size LRU, or count keys and shed when the cap is hit) rather than relying only on age; validate the username against `USERNAME` *before* keying anything on it; and put the first line of defence at the edge (WAF/LB connection and request-rate limits), because an in-process limiter is itself a target and dies with the process that needs protecting. Apply the same request limit to `GET /login`, which currently allocates a session per hit.\n\n### SEC-04: HTML responses carry no `X-Frame-Options`, CSP, `X-Content-Type-Options` or `Referrer-Policy`; no HSTS\n\n**Severity:** Medium\n\n**CWE:** CWE-693: Protection Mechanism Failure\n\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Responses.java:17-33`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Responses.java:17-33\n    static void doNotCache(HttpServletResponse response) {\n        response.setHeader(\"Cache-Control\", \"no-store\");\n        response.setHeader(\"Pragma\", \"no-cache\");\n        response.setDateHeader(\"Expires\", 0L);\n    }\n\n    static void html(HttpServletResponse response, int status, String bodyHtml) throws IOException {\n        doNotCache(response);\n        response.setStatus(status);\n        response.setContentType(\"text/html;charset=UTF-8\");\n        try (PrintWriter out = response.getWriter()) {\n            out.print(\"<!DOCTYPE html>\\n<html lang=\\\"en\\\">\\n<head>\\n<meta charset=\\\"UTF-8\\\">\\n\");\n            out.print(\"<meta name=\\\"viewport\\\" content=\\\"width=device-width, initial-scale=1\\\">\\n\");\n            out.print(\"<title>\" + Html.escape(pageTitle(bodyHtml)) + \"</title>\\n\");\n            out.print(STYLE + \"</head>\\n<body>\\n\" + bodyHtml + \"\\n</body>\\n</html>\\n\");\n        }\n    }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `no-store` is the only header the HTML path sets. The JSON path at least adds `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` (`:39`), which shows the omission on the HTML side is an oversight rather than a decision. The practical gaps on a cookie-authenticated login page are: no `X-Frame-Options: DENY` and no CSP `frame-ancestors 'none'`, so any origin can frame `/login` and run a clickjacking or drag-and-drop credential-capture attack against a signed-out victim, with `SameSite=Lax` no protection because the framed request is a same-site top-level navigation from the *victim's* browser; no CSP at all, so there is no defence-in-depth if an escaping bug ever appears in the `Html.escape`/`Html.attribute` sinks (there is no finding against them today — see §6 — but CSP is what makes the next such bug non-exploitable); no `X-Content-Type-Options`, which matters if the container ever serves an uploaded or error-generated document with a sniffable type; and no `Strict-Transport-Security`, which is the downgrade-protection half of SEC-02. The page reflects the submitted username back into the document (`LoginServlet.formHtml`), so this is a page an attacker's content reaches, not a static one. `Referrer-Policy` is absent too: a link out of `/account` leaks the session-bearing URL path to third parties. Individually each header is a hardening layer, which is why this is Medium rather than High; collectively, an authenticated application serving HTML with only cache headers has no browser-side safety net at all.\n\n**Fix:** Set the whole set once, in the single writer, so no new endpoint can forget it:\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Responses.java (corrected)\n    static void doNotCache(HttpServletResponse response) {\n        response.setHeader(\"Cache-Control\", \"no-store\");\n        response.setHeader(\"Pragma\", \"no-cache\");\n        response.setDateHeader(\"Expires\", 0L);\n    }\n\n    /** Browser-side protections for every HTML document this application serves. */\n    static void securityHeaders(HttpServletResponse response, boolean overTls) {\n        // The page embeds one inline <style> block (STYLE above) and loads nothing else.\n        response.setHeader(\"Content-Security-Policy\",\n                \"default-src 'none'; script-src 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'; \"\n                        + \"base-uri 'none'; form-action 'self'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'\");\n        response.setHeader(\"X-Content-Type-Options\", \"nosniff\");\n        response.setHeader(\"X-Frame-Options\", \"DENY\");               // legacy browsers\n        response.setHeader(\"Referrer-Policy\", \"same-origin\");\n        response.setHeader(\"Permissions-Policy\", \"geolocation=(), camera=(), microphone=()\");\n        if (overTls) {\n            response.setHeader(\"Strict-Transport-Security\",\n                    \"max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains\");\n        }\n    }\n\n    static void html(HttpServletResponse response, int status, String bodyHtml, boolean overTls)\n            throws IOException {\n        doNotCache(response);\n        securityHeaders(response, overTls);\n        // ... unchanged body writing ...\n    }\n```\n\nAdd `nosniff` to `json()` as well (already present at `:39`) and emit HSTS from the TLS connector or edge proxy as in SEC-02, so the header exists even for responses this class does not write.\n\n### SEC-05: Log forging: unvalidated, length-unbounded username written to the application log\n\n**Severity:** Medium\n\n**CWE:** CWE-117: Improper Output Neutralization for Logs\n\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java:112-115`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java:112-115\n            if (!credentialsOk) {\n                context.throttle().recordFailure(throttleKey, Instant.now());\n                LOG.log(Level.FINE, \"Rejected sign-in for user ''{0}'' from {1}\",\n                        new Object[] {username, request.getRemoteAddr()});\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `username` is attacker-controlled and reaches the logger without validation: `trimToEmpty` (`:187-189`) strips outer whitespace and nothing else, and the `USERNAME` check at `:97` decides only whether to *look up* the account — it never stops the value being logged, because the refusal branch at `:101-107` falls through to this line for invalid usernames too. `MessageFormat` quoting (`''{0}''`) protects the message pattern, not the log line. Consequences, in increasing order of cost: a CR/LF in the parameter forges additional log entries with attacker-chosen timestamps and text, letting someone fabricate sign-in attempts for an innocent account or hide a real attack in noise; a quote or bracket breaks whatever parser sits downstream of the appender; there is no length bound on the value at all, so a single request can write an arbitrarily large record into the log pipeline; and because log files are commonly rendered in SIEM dashboards, greps and web UIs, an attacker who later obtains any reflected view of the log gets an injection point there. The Tomcat access log has a companion weakness — `tools/tomcat-env.sh:89-90` records `%r`, the raw request line, and Tomcat's quoting of control characters there is the container's, not this code's — so the same username arrives unsanitised through a second channel. Note this is the integrity-of-evidence problem, not a disclosure one: nothing here leaks a secret, which is why it is Medium.\n\n**Fix:** Log the *validated* identity and nothing else, and bound what you do log:\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java (corrected)\n            if (!credentialsOk) {\n                context.throttle().recordFailure(throttleKey, Instant.now());\n                // Only a value that matched USERNAME is ever written out; everything else is\n                // replaced by a fixed token, so no attacker byte reaches the log stream.\n                String logName = usernameValid(username) ? username : \"<rejected>\";\n                LOG.log(Level.FINE, \"Rejected sign-in for user ''{0}'' from {1}\",\n                        new Object[] {logName, request.getRemoteAddr()});\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java (corrected, username intake)\n    private static String trimToEmpty(String value) {\n        if (value == null) {\n            return \"\";\n        }\n        // Bound the value before it is used as a key, a log field, or a form value.\n        String trimmed = value.trim();\n        return trimmed.length() > MAX_USERNAME_CHARS ? trimmed.substring(0, MAX_USERNAME_CHARS)\n                                                     : trimmed;\n    }\n```\n\nAdditionally, configure the appender for the deployment's log format (or use a JSON appender that escapes structurally rather than by convention), and treat the access log as untrusted input in dashboards because `%r` reflects the request line.\n\n### SEC-06: Lockout keyed only on (IP, username): distributed spraying is unthrottled, and a shared source IP is a lockout DoS\n\n**Severity:** Medium\n\n**CWE:** CWE-307: Improper Restriction of Excessive Authentication Attempts\n\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java:66-68`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java:65-68\n    /** A key that identifies one caller chasing one account name. */\n    public static String keyFor(String remoteAddr, String username) {\n        return remoteAddr + \"\\n\" + username;\n    }\n```\n\nThe state behind that key is per-process and pruned only by age (`:44`, `:48`, `:122-132`):\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java:44-48\n    private final Map<String, Attempt> attempts = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();\n\n    /** Wall clock of the last sweep; pruning is throttled to once a minute. */\n    private volatile Instant lastPurgeAt = Instant.EPOCH;\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** The design protects the *pair*, which is the wrong subject for both of the threats a lockout exists to handle. For the attacker: the pair space is enormous, so a spray over many usernames from many source addresses never accumulates five failures against any single key — an adversary holding 500 hosts and one target account can try thousands of passwords with each attempt still arriving at a fresh budget. (The username-variation half of the same weakness is SEC-03, where the payoff is CPU rather than credentials.) For the legitimate user: the same keying makes the lockout trivially weaponisable against the site — anyone behind a shared egress address (corporate NAT, campus, university, mobile carrier CGNAT, a large VPN pool) can lock their neighbours out of *their own* accounts simply by failing five logons as `victim@example.com`, and `verdictFor` (`:70-82`) then returns 429 for everyone else sharing `remoteAddr` for the next 15 minutes. That is denial of service against authentication that an unauthenticated stranger can trigger at will. Two structural problems remain: state lives in one JVM's `ConcurrentHashMap`, so behind a load balancer an attacker sprays per-node budgets and a restart erases every lockout, and `remoteAddr` is `request.getRemoteAddr()` (`LoginServlet.java:76`), which is the immediate socket peer — the attacker's chosen source IP when there is no trusted proxy, and trivially rotated when there is one.\n\n**Fix:** Split the two limits, put one on the account and one on the caller, and use a signal the attacker cannot rotate for free:\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java (corrected)\n    /** Account-facing limit: keyed on the normalised username only, and enforced cluster-wide. */\n    public static String accountKey(String normalisedUsername) {\n        return \"acct:\" + normalisedUsername;\n    }\n\n    /** Caller-facing request limit: keyed on the trusted network identity, not on a username\n     *  the caller invented, and never used to deny a *different* user's own sign-in. */\n    public static String callerKey(String callerAddress) {\n        return \"ip:\" + (isIpv6(callerAddress)\n                ? prefix64(callerAddress)          // one /64 is one subscriber, one /32 one host\n                : callerAddress);\n    }\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java (corrected)\n        // Per-account lockout is a shared-state decision: a second instance must see it, and a\n        // restart must not erase it. Redis/memcached/DB, not an in-process map.\n        if (!context.throttle().accountLockedOut(subject)) {\n            // A per-caller *rate* limit is not an account lockout: it throttles the source, it\n            // never locks an innocent neighbour out of their own account.\n            ...\n        }\n```\n\nThen: never lock an account solely because of a shared source IP — instead escalate friction (CAPTCHA, step-up, notification to the account holder) and lock the *account* on its own failure history with alerting; back the counters with shared storage; derive the caller address only from a configured trusted-proxy hop; and treat this throttle as one layer behind edge rate limiting, per the rule that rate limiting must not be the sole defence."}}],"stop_reason":"tool_use","usage":{"input_tokens":155849,"output_tokens":4853,"cache_read_input_tokens":0,"total_tokens":160702}}},{"type":"user","uuid":"becdad3a-ba6d-4925-a624-0ac9ce4dd015","session_id":"45c8944e-d3f6-41a6-ac2d-c70f0846fd52","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_b5253bc65f0a47898a29483f","is_error":false,"content":"The file: /data/results-cyber/qwen/set_a/sallie/1/basic/security_review.md has been updated. The throttle map is bounded in *lifetime* — `purgeStale` (`:122-132`) drops entries older than window + lockout, roughly 30 minutes, at most once a minute — so this is exhaustion by cardinality and key size inside that window, not an unbounded leak; that is still enough, because the CPU cost, not the memory, is what takes the site down first. `recordFailure` is already `compute`-atomic (`:94-100`), so none of this is a counter race; it is a missing limit in front of an intentionally expensive operation.\n\n**Fix:** Limit *requests*, not failures, and key the limit on something the attacker does not control. Per-account failure counting stays (it is what stops password guessing), but it can never be the throttle that protects CPU:\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java (corrected)\n        String username = trimToEmpty(request.getParameter(\"username\"));\n        // Normalise and bound BEFORE the value is used as a cache/throttle key or logged.\n        String subject = LoginThrottle.normaliseUsername(username);   // lowercase, <= 64, else \"\"\n        String throttleKey = LoginThrottle.keyFor(callerAddress(request), subject);\n\n        // Cost guard, independent of the failure budget: no attacker-chosen string can\n        // mint an unlimited number of full-cost stretching operations.\n        if (!context.limiter().allowLoginAttempt(callerAddress(request))) {\n            Responses.doNotCache(response);\n            response.setIntHeader(\"Retry-After\", LoginThrottle.RETRY_AFTER_SECONDS);\n            Responses.html(response, HttpServletResponse.SC_TOO_MANY_REQUESTS,\n                    formHtml(GENERIC_FAILURE, \"\", false, token, prefix(request)));\n            return;\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java (corrected)\n    /** Fixed-length, normalised identity: `Alice.` and `alice_` must not mint separate budgets. */\n    public static String normaliseUsername(String username) {\n        if (username == null || !USERNAME.matcher(username).matches()) {\n            return \"\";\n        }\n        return username.toLowerCase(java.util.Locale.ROOT);\n    }\n\n    /** Two limits: a per-caller request rate and a global ceiling that protects the KDF pool. */\n    public boolean allowLoginAttempt(String callerAddress) {\n        Instant now = Instant.now();\n        boolean perCaller = tryAcquire(callerKey(callerAddress), LOGIN_ATTEMPTS_PER_MINUTE, now);\n        boolean global = globalAdmissions.tryAcquire(now);\n        return perCaller && global;   // either exhausted => refuse before any stretching happens\n    }\n```\n\nAlso: bound the throttle map's cardinality (fixed-size LRU, or count keys and shed when the cap is hit) rather than relying only on age; validate the username against `USERNAME` *before* keying anything on it; and put the first line of defence at the edge (WAF/LB connection and request-rate limits), because an in-process limiter is itself a target and dies with the process that needs protecting. Apply the same request limit to `GET /login`, which currently allocates a session per hit.\n\n### SEC-04: HTML responses carry no `X-Frame-Options`, CSP, `X-Content-Type-Options` or `Referrer-Policy`; no HSTS\n\n**Severity:** Medium\n\n**CWE:** CWE-693: Protection Mechanism Failure\n\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Responses.java:17-33`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Responses.java:17-33\n    static void doNotCache(HttpServletResponse response) {\n        response.setHeader(\"Cache-Control\", \"no-store\");\n        response.setHeader(\"Pragma\", \"no-cache\");\n        response.setDateHeader(\"Expires\", 0L);\n    }\n\n    static void html(HttpServletResponse response, int status, String bodyHtml) throws IOException {\n        doNotCache(response);\n        response.setStatus(status);\n        response.setContentType(\"text/html;charset=UTF-8\");\n        try (PrintWriter out = response.getWriter()) {\n            out.print(\"<!DOCTYPE html>\\n<html lang=\\\"en\\\">\\n<head>\\n<meta charset=\\\"UTF-8\\\">\\n\");\n            out.print(\"<meta name=\\\"viewport\\\" content=\\\"width=device-width, initial-scale=1\\\">\\n\");\n            out.print(\"<title>\" + Html.escape(pageTitle(bodyHtml)) + \"</title>\\n\");\n            out.print(STYLE + \"</head>\\n<body>\\n\" + bodyHtml + \"\\n</body>\\n</html>\\n\");\n        }\n    }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `no-store` is the only header the HTML path sets. The JSON path at least adds `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` (`:39`), which shows the omission on the HTML side is an oversight rather than a decision. The practical gaps on a cookie-authenticated login page are: no `X-Frame-Options: DENY` and no CSP `frame-ancestors 'none'`, so any origin can frame `/login` and run a clickjacking or drag-and-drop credential-capture attack against a signed-out victim, with `SameSite=Lax` no protection because the framed request is a same-site top-level navigation from the *victim's* browser; no CSP at all, so there is no defence-in-depth if an escaping bug ever appears in the `Html.escape`/`Html.attribute` sinks (there is no finding against them today — see §6 — but CSP is what makes the next such bug non-exploitable); no `X-Content-Type-Options`, which matters if the container ever serves an uploaded or error-generated document with a sniffable type; and no `Strict-Transport-Security`, which is the downgrade-protection half of SEC-02. The page reflects the submitted username back into the document (`LoginServlet.formHtml`), so this is a page an attacker's content reaches, not a static one. `Referrer-Policy` is absent too: a link out of `/account` leaks the session-bearing URL path to third parties. Individually each header is a hardening layer, which is why this is Medium rather than High; collectively, an authenticated application serving HTML with only cache headers has no browser-side safety net at all.\n\n**Fix:** Set the whole set once, in the single writer, so no new endpoint can forget it:\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/Responses.java (corrected)\n    static void doNotCache(HttpServletResponse response) {\n        response.setHeader(\"Cache-Control\", \"no-store\");\n        response.setHeader(\"Pragma\", \"no-cache\");\n        response.setDateHeader(\"Expires\", 0L);\n    }\n\n    /** Browser-side protections for every HTML document this application serves. */\n    static void securityHeaders(HttpServletResponse response, boolean overTls) {\n        // The page embeds one inline <style> block (STYLE above) and loads nothing else.\n        response.setHeader(\"Content-Security-Policy\",\n                \"default-src 'none'; script-src 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'; \"\n                        + \"base-uri 'none'; form-action 'self'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'\");\n        response.setHeader(\"X-Content-Type-Options\", \"nosniff\");\n        response.setHeader(\"X-Frame-Options\", \"DENY\");               // legacy browsers\n        response.setHeader(\"Referrer-Policy\", \"same-origin\");\n        response.setHeader(\"Permissions-Policy\", \"geolocation=(), camera=(), microphone=()\");\n        if (overTls) {\n            response.setHeader(\"Strict-Transport-Security\",\n                    \"max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains\");\n        }\n    }\n\n    static void html(HttpServletResponse response, int status, String bodyHtml, boolean overTls)\n            throws IOException {\n        doNotCache(response);\n        securityHeaders(response, overTls);\n        // ... unchanged body writing ...\n    }\n```\n\nAdd `nosniff` to `json()` as well (already present at `:39`) and emit HSTS from the TLS connector or edge proxy as in SEC-02, so the header exists even for responses this class does not write.\n\n### SEC-05: Log forging: unvalidated, length-unbounded username written to the application log\n\n**Severity:** Medium\n\n**CWE:** CWE-117: Improper Output Neutralization for Logs\n\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java:112-115`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java:112-115\n            if (!credentialsOk) {\n                context.throttle().recordFailure(throttleKey, Instant.now());\n                LOG.log(Level.FINE, \"Rejected sign-in for user ''{0}'' from {1}\",\n                        new Object[] {username, request.getRemoteAddr()});\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `username` is attacker-controlled and reaches the logger without validation: `trimToEmpty` (`:187-189`) strips outer whitespace and nothing else, and the `USERNAME` check at `:97` decides only whether to *look up* the account — it never stops the value being logged, because the refusal branch at `:101-107` falls through to this line for invalid usernames too. `MessageFormat` quoting (`''{0}''`) protects the message pattern, not the log line. Consequences, in increasing order of cost: a CR/LF in the parameter forges additional log entries with attacker-chosen timestamps and text, letting someone fabricate sign-in attempts for an innocent account or hide a real attack in noise; a quote or bracket breaks whatever parser sits downstream of the appender; there is no length bound on the value at all, so a single request can write an arbitrarily large record into the log pipeline; and because log files are commonly rendered in SIEM dashboards, greps and web UIs, an attacker who later obtains any reflected view of the log gets an injection point there. The Tomcat access log has a companion weakness — `tools/tomcat-env.sh:89-90` records `%r`, the raw request line, and Tomcat's quoting of control characters there is the container's, not this code's — so the same username arrives unsanitised through a second channel. Note this is the integrity-of-evidence problem, not a disclosure one: nothing here leaks a secret, which is why it is Medium.\n\n**Fix:** Log the *validated* identity and nothing else, and bound what you do log:\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java (corrected)\n            if (!credentialsOk) {\n                context.throttle().recordFailure(throttleKey, Instant.now());\n                // Only a value that matched USERNAME is ever written out; everything else is\n                // replaced by a fixed token, so no attacker byte reaches the log stream.\n                String logName = usernameValid(username) ? username : \"<rejected>\";\n                LOG.log(Level.FINE, \"Rejected sign-in for user ''{0}'' from {1}\",\n                        new Object[] {logName, request.getRemoteAddr()});\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java (corrected, username intake)\n    private static String trimToEmpty(String value) {\n        if (value == null) {\n            return \"\";\n        }\n        // Bound the value before it is used as a key, a log field, or a form value.\n        String trimmed = value.trim();\n        return trimmed.length() > MAX_USERNAME_CHARS ? trimmed.substring(0, MAX_USERNAME_CHARS)\n                                                     : trimmed;\n    }\n```\n\nAdditionally, configure the appender for the deployment's log format (or use a JSON appender that escapes structurally rather than by convention), and treat the access log as untrusted input in dashboards because `%r` reflects the request line.\n\n### SEC-06: Lockout keyed only on (IP, username): distributed spraying is unthrottled, and a shared source IP is a lockout DoS\n\n**Severity:** Medium\n\n**CWE:** CWE-307: Improper Restriction of Excessive Authentication Attempts\n\n**Location:** `src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java:66-68`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java:65-68\n    /** A key that identifies one caller chasing one account name. */\n    public static String keyFor(String remoteAddr, String username) {\n        return remoteAddr + \"\\n\" + username;\n    }\n```\n\nThe state behind that key is per-process and pruned only by age (`:44`, `:48`, `:122-132`):\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java:44-48\n    private final Map<String, Attempt> attempts = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();\n\n    /** Wall clock of the last sweep; pruning is throttled to once a minute. */\n    private volatile Instant lastPurgeAt = Instant.EPOCH;\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** The design protects the *pair*, which is the wrong subject for both of the threats a lockout exists to handle. For the attacker: the pair space is enormous, so a spray over many usernames from many source addresses never accumulates five failures against any single key — an adversary holding 500 hosts and one target account can try thousands of passwords with each attempt still arriving at a fresh budget. (The username-variation half of the same weakness is SEC-03, where the payoff is CPU rather than credentials.) For the legitimate user: the same keying makes the lockout trivially weaponisable against the site — anyone behind a shared egress address (corporate NAT, campus, university, mobile carrier CGNAT, a large VPN pool) can lock their neighbours out of *their own* accounts simply by failing five logons as `victim@example.com`, and `verdictFor` (`:70-82`) then returns 429 for everyone else sharing `remoteAddr` for the next 15 minutes. That is denial of service against authentication that an unauthenticated stranger can trigger at will. Two structural problems remain: state lives in one JVM's `ConcurrentHashMap`, so behind a load balancer an attacker sprays per-node budgets and a restart erases every lockout, and `remoteAddr` is `request.getRemoteAddr()` (`LoginServlet.java:76`), which is the immediate socket peer — the attacker's chosen source IP when there is no trusted proxy, and trivially rotated when there is one.\n\n**Fix:** Split the two limits, put one on the account and one on the caller, and use a signal the attacker cannot rotate for free:\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginThrottle.java (corrected)\n    /** Account-facing limit: keyed on the normalised username only, and enforced cluster-wide. */\n    public static String accountKey(String normalisedUsername) {\n        return \"acct:\" + normalisedUsername;\n    }\n\n    /** Caller-facing request limit: keyed on the trusted network identity, not on a username\n     *  the caller invented, and never used to deny a *different* user's own sign-in. */\n    public static String callerKey(String callerAddress) {\n        return \"ip:\" + (isIpv6(callerAddress)\n                ? prefix64(callerAddress)          // one /64 is one subscriber, one /32 one host\n                : callerAddress);\n    }\n```\n\n```java\n// src/main/java/com/example/auth/web/LoginServlet.java (corrected)\n        // Per-account lockout is a shared-state decision: a second instance must see it, and a\n        // restart must not erase it. Redis/memcached/DB, not an in-process map.\n        if (!context.throttle().accountLockedOut(subject)) {\n            // A per-caller *rate* limit is not an account lockout: it throttles the source, it\n            // never locks an innocent neighbour out of their own account.\n            ...\n        }\n```\n\nThen: never lock an account solely because of a shared source IP — instead escalate friction (CAPTCHA, step-up, notification to the account holder) and lock the *account* on its own failure history with alerting; back the counters with shared storage; derive the caller address only from a configured trusted-proxy hop; and treat this throttle as one layer behind edge rate limiting, per the rule that rate limiting must not be the sole defence.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"de888a2d-b800-4fdc-a056-364c7edd1c61","session_id":"45c8944e-d3f6-41a6-ac2d-c70f0846fd52","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"de888a2d-b800-4fdc-a056-364c7edd1c61","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Now SEC-07 through SEC-11. Each of those is the answer an attacker is otherwise probing for: the iteration count tells them the price of an offline crack and confirms they can read `web.xml`'s policy back to them; the throttle triple tells them precisely how many wrong passwords they may send per window per key and how long to wait, and lets them verify a lockout is armed before they burn the account they wanted to keep. On a JVM without `ZeroingByte`/ARC4-style cleanup, that array survives until the collector reclaims it — reachable to anyone who can read a heap dump (`jcmd GC.heap_dump`, `-XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError`, a core dump, or swap), and this path runs on *every* refused attempt, so the demo's own brute-force sequence leaves 5+ such arrays behind. `verify`'s discipline proves the author intended this class to leave no key material in memory; the equal-cost path is where it leaks. Note the derived value here is a key for a record that never matches anything (the timing reference), so what leaks is the stretched form of a *candidate*, not of a stored password — hence Low.\n\nFix:\n```java\n    public void chargeRefusedAttempt(char[] password) {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(password, \"password\");\n        byte[] digest = derive(password, timingReference.salt(), timingReference.iterations());\n        try {\n            // The value is deliberately discarded -- but \"discarded\" has to mean wiped, exactly as\n            // verify() does, or the stretched form of a live candidate sits on the heap until GC.\n        } finally {\n            if (digest != null) {\n                Arrays.fill(digest, (byte) 0);\n            }\n        }\n    }\n```\nHmm, an empty try is odd. Better:\n```java\n    public void chargeRefusedAttempt(char[] password) {\n        Objects.requireNonNull(password, \"password\");\n        byte[] digest = derive(password, timingReference.salt(), timingReference.iterations());\n        if (digest != null) {\n            Arrays.fill(digest, (byte) 0);\n        }\n    }\n```\nThat's clean and mirrors verify. Add note: or have `derive` take a `Consumer`/return nothing for this path. Also: same cleanup belongs in `isUsableCandidate`-driven paths? Keep it simple. And mention: a stronger structural fix is a `deriveInto` that writes into a caller-supplied buffer, or returning `SecretKey` from a try-with-resources — but the one-line wipe matches the existing style.\n\nNow §6. Verified `newId()` and `createFormToken()` both draw 32 bytes from the same `SecureRandom` field, and the encoder is URL-safe; the ids are therefore neither enumerable nor encoding-sensitive in a cookie.\n\n**Session-fixation** — the pre-login record is invalidated *before* the new id is minted (`LoginServlet.java:132-133`), and `SessionStore.invalidate` deletes the record and marks it revoked, so the id the sign-in page was handed cannot resolve afterwards even though it was never attacker-supplied. Checked both `completeSession` and `showForm` (`:151`) and confirmed the store has no path that re-mints an id without invalidating the old record.\n\n**Constant-time comparison of every secret** — `MessageDigest.isEqual` for the password digest (`PasswordService.java:120`), for stored-record comparison (`:217-218`), and for the session/form-token lookup in the store (`SessionStore.java:139`). No `equals`/`Arrays.equals`/`==` on any secret anywhere; I searched each comparison site rather than trusting one.\n\n**Cookie input is validated before it reaches the store** — `Cookies.java:20-21` declares the id shape (`[A-Za-z0-9_-]{32,128}`) and `readSessionId` (`:27-44`) applies it and returns `null` on anything else, so `SessionStore.resolve` is never called with attacker-chosen bytes. URL rewriting is disabled by `<tracking-mode>COOKIE</tracking-mode>` (`web.xml:80`), so no session id can ever appear in a URL where it would land in a referer, proxy log, or browser history — the one thing that would have made the whole design leakier than it needs to be. I found no authenticated page renderable without `no-store`.\n\n**Account enumeration** — the response is the same `GENERIC_FAILURE` string for unknown username, malformed username, bad token, wrong password, and throttled-but-not-yet-locked (`LoginServlet.java:16`, `:88-107`, `:116`), and the refused paths pay the same 240,000-iteration cost as a real attempt via `chargeRefusedAttempt` (`:101-107`), so neither the message nor the timing separates the cases. `tests/end-to-end.sh:153-166` measures the equal-cost claim over six samples with a 45 % tolerance. (The residue is the log/username asymmetry in SEC-05.)\n\n**Default-deny authorisation** — `AuthFilter` is mapped to `/*` (`web.xml:84-86`), `OPEN_PATHS` is a fixed `Set` of three non-secret paths (`AuthFilter.java:36`), everything else requires a session resolved through the validated cookie (`:60-64`), and `isOpen` (`:91-93`) matches exact paths only — no suffix or pattern matching that a `/status/..;/` style path could slip through. `AccountServlet` re-checks the session and returns 401 with `WWW-Authenticate` (`:39-46`) so defence is not solely the filter's, and `SessionStore` refuses any session past either timeout at resolve time. `LogoutServlet` and `LoginServlet` each resolve their own session rather than trusting an upstream attribute.\n\n**Password-record integrity** — a `StoredPassword` cannot be constructed or parsed into something the verifier will accept by accident: algorithm name must equal `PBKDF2_SHA256`, salt must be ≥16 bytes, digest must be exactly 32 bytes, iteration bounds enforced (`PasswordService.java:172-181`, `:197-208`), and `toString()` redacts the salt and digest (`:227`) so a record cannot reach a log line whole. The KDF itself uses 16-byte random salt from `SecureRandom` (`:23`, `:28`) and 32-byte output (`:24`), with the deployment's 240,000 iterations declared in `web.xml:17-20` and read at `AuthContext.java:57`. (Only the acceptance floor is weak → SEC-07.)\n\n**Password handling in memory** — passwords are carried as `char[]` and cleared in a `finally` (`LoginServlet.java:123`), `InMemoryUserStore` copies and wipes its working buffer (`:32-37`, `:46`), and `derive` clears the `PBEKeySpec` (`PasswordService.java:151`). No `String` password is created from request input anywhere (the one `String` at `LoginServlet.java:108` is the candidate passed to the KDF and is itself a `char[]`). Every JSON value passes `Json.quote` (`Json.java:13-25`) — string fields are written only as `Json.field(name, value)` pairs, `AuthFilter`'s 401 reflects the request path through `Json.quote` (`AuthFilter.java:74-76`), and `Json.field(String,String)` throws on a null value instead of emitting a bare `null` (`Json.java:24`), so a missing value cannot produce malformed JSON that a client mis-parses. Content-Type is always `charset=UTF-8` (`Responses.java:28`, `:37`), closing the UTF-7/charset-confusion variant. I found no sink that concatenates request data into markup: the login page, account page, and refusals are all built through the two helpers. No XSS finding.\n\n**Session-store bounds** — the constructor rejects an `absoluteTimeout` shorter than `idleTimeout` (`SessionStore.java:45-54`), so a misconfigured `web.xml` fails at startup instead of silently minting sessions that outlive their own cap, and the form-token TTL is capped by the idle timeout (`MAX_FORM_TOKEN_TTL`), so a long session cannot leave a sign-in token valid for days.\n\n**Build and test hygiene** — `build.sh:27` compiles with `-Xlint:all -Werror --release 17`, so a new unchecked or deprecated auth call fails the build; `tests/unit-checks.sh` compiles the checks with `-Werror` too and runs them with assertions enabled (`java -ea`), and the WAR layout is verified after packaging (`build.sh:42-53`). `tests/end-to-end.sh` asserts the behaviours that would otherwise regress silently: single-use token, refusal of a forged id, timing equalisation, lockout scoping to one username only, and that no salt or digest appears in `/status` output. the algorithm name, salt length and digest length are all re-checked on every record at `PasswordService.java:172-181` and `:197-208`). The weakness is the *acceptance* bound. `MIN_ITERATIONS` is the floor a stored record must clear to be believed, and at 1,000 it sits 240× below the deployment's own policy and orders of magnitude below current guidance for PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, which is in the hundreds of thousands. So a record that claims one thousand iterations is not rejected as anomalous — it parses, it passes `StoredPassword`'s constructor, and `verify` will happily stretch an attacker's guess 1,000 times against it instead of 240,000. Any path that lets a record be written or downgraded — a data import, a restore from an older backup, an administrator hand-editing a store, a future feature that persists a record from a request body — turns that into a 240× cheaper offline crack for exactly the accounts an attacker cares about, and the check that was supposed to catch it is the one that waved it through. The ceiling at `MAX_ITERATIONS` is doing real work in the other direction (a record cannot be used to DoS the verifier with a billion iterations); it is the floor that is decorative. Severity is Low rather than higher only because the store in this tree is `InMemoryUserStore`, whose records are fixed at compile time, so there is today no write path to downgrade; the finding is about the validator that will exist the moment there is one.\n\n- **Fix:** Make the floor equal the policy, so a cheap record is an error rather than a legitimate legacy format, and upgrade anything below the target on next successful sign-in:\n\n  ```java\n  // The floor must equal the policy: a record below it is not acceptable legacy data, it is a\n  // 240x-cheaper hash to crack. Records below the target are re-derived on next login; records\n  // below the floor are rejected outright and the user is sent to a password reset.\n  static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 240_000;\n  static final int TARGET_ITERATIONS = 240_000;\n  static final int MAX_ITERATIONS = 5_000_000;\n  ```\n\n  ```java\n  /** True when a valid stored record is below today's cost target and must be re-derived. */\n  public boolean needsRehash(StoredPassword stored) {\n      return stored == null || stored.iterations() < TARGET_ITERATIONS;\n  }\n  ```\n\n  In `LoginServlet`, after `verify` returns true, re-derive the record at `TARGET_ITERATIONS` with a fresh salt and replace it in the `UserStore`. Raise the target as hardware gets faster, and when you do, keep `MIN_ITERATIONS` one *rehash-cycle* behind it at most — never three orders of magnitude behind — and when the accepted set does need to lag for migration reasons, make the lag explicit and observable (`log.warning` naming the account, plus a metric) instead of silent. The iteration count stays inside the stored record rather than in configuration, so this remains a safe rolling upgrade for existing hashes.\n\n---\n\n### SEC-08: Hardcoded TLS keystore password and untrusted certificate in the Tomcat provisioning script\n\n- **Severity:** Low\n- **CWE:** CWE-259: Use of Hard-coded Password\n- **Location:** `tools/tomcat-env.sh:59-62`\n- **Vulnerable code:**\n\n  ```sh\n  # tools/tomcat-env.sh:59-62\n    keytool -genkeypair -alias demo -keyalg RSA -keysize 2048 \\\n            -dname \"CN=localhost, O=throwaway demo, C=US\" \\\n            -validity 2 -storepass changeit -keypass changeit \\\n            -keystore \"$keystore\" -storetype PKCS12 >/dev/null 2>&1\n  ```\n\n- **Why it is a problem:** The private key that terminates the HTTPS listener is protected by a passphrase that is committed to the repository, identical on every machine, and known to anyone who can read the script. The keystore itself is written to `$CATALINA_BASE/demo.jks` with default permissions, so the passphrase is the *only* thing standing between \"someone with read access to that directory\" and \"someone who can impersonate the origin on the TLS port and decrypt or forge anything the demo serves over HTTPS\" — and a public passphrase protects nothing. The same literal is repeated at `tools/tomcat-env.sh:83`, inside the generated `server.xml` (`certificateKeystorePassword=\"changeit\"`), so the credential is duplicated in two files and in the running Tomcat configuration. I am weighing this as a *credential* problem, not as \"the HTTPS path looks configurable\": the certificate is freshly generated per run (`:54-57` explains why — the cookie's `Secure` attribute is only observable over TLS), which is good practice; the mistake is bolting it with a constant. The second half of the problem is the trust story. The certificate is self-signed, `CN=localhost`, valid for 2 days, and the test suite reaches it with `curl -k`/`--insecure`, which switches certificate validation off rather than adding the demo CA to the trust store. `tests/end-to-end.sh` therefore proves the connector answers on TLS without ever proving the peer identity is checked — and every assertion that would catch SEC-02's downgrade rides on that same unchecked channel. `tools/tomcat-env.sh:78-79` compounds it: the HTTP connector carries only `redirectPort`, so an HTTPS URL reached over plain HTTP is delivered as a 302 that a stripping adversary can intercept (SEC-02), and there is nothing on either connector enforcing TLS 1.2+ or a safe cipher-suite order; the demo inherits Tomcat defaults, so no TLS configuration is actually being exercised or asserted anywhere.\n\n- **Fix:** Generate a fresh passphrase per run, pass it through the environment rather than the command line or the file, and make Tomcat resolve it the same way:\n\n  ```sh\n    local keystore=\"$CATALINA_BASE/demo.jks\"\n    # Fresh random passphrase per run and a key file nobody else can read. Never a literal: the\n    # passphrase is the only protection on the HTTPS private key.\n    export DEMO_KEYSTORE_PASSWORD=\"$(umask 077; openssl rand -base64 24 | tr -d '\\n')\"\n    keytool -genkeypair -alias demo -keyalg RSA -keysize 2048 \\\n            -dname \"CN=localhost, O=throwaway demo, C=US\" \\\n            -validity 2 -storepass:env DEMO_KEYSTORE_PASSWORD \\\n            -keypass:env DEMO_KEYSTORE_PASSWORD \\\n            -keystore \"$keystore\" -storetype PKCS12 >/dev/null 2>&1\n    chmod 600 \"$keystore\" 2>/dev/null || true\n  ```\n\n  ```xml\n        <Certificate certificateKeystoreFile=\"$keystore\"\n                     certificateKeystorePassword=\"${env.DEMO_KEYSTORE_PASSWORD}\"\n                     type=\"RSA\"/>\n  ```\n\n  Then finish the TLS story on the same connector, and never let a test disable validation:\n\n  ```xml\n      <Connector port=\"$HTTPS_PORT\" protocol=\"org.apache.coyote.http11.Http11NioProtocol\"\n                 executor=\"demo\" SSLEnabled=\"true\" scheme=\"https\" secure=\"true\"\n                 sslProtocol=\"TLS\" tlsMinVersion=\"1.2\" useServerCipherSuiteOrder=\"true\"\n                 hsts=\"on\" hstsMaxAge=\"31536000\" hstsIncludeSubDomains=\"true\">\n  ```\n\n  ```sh\n  # tests/end-to-end.sh — trust the generated certificate instead of switching checks off\n  keytool -exportcert -alias demo -rfc -keystore \"$KEYSTORE\" \\\n          -storepass:env DEMO_KEYSTORE_PASSWORD > \"$WORK/demo-ca.pem\"\n  HTTPS_BASE=\"https://localhost:$HTTPS_PORT$CONTEXT\"\n  run curl -sS --cacert \"$WORK/demo-ca.pem\" --resolve \"localhost:$HTTPS_PORT:127.0.0.1\" \\\n          \"$HTTPS_BASE/status\"\n  ```\n\n  If validating a locally generated certificate is genuinely impractical in this harness, keep `-k` but say in one comment line why, and stop treating TLS as covered while it is. For anything that outlives a single demo run, use a real ACME certificate: `certbot --nginx -d auth.example.com --redirect --strict-sni` and let the ACME HTTP-01 challenge be the only listener on port 80.\n\n---\n\n### SEC-09: Session identifiers persisted on disk and the password exposed in process arguments\n\n- **Severity:** Low\n- **CWE:** CWE-313: Cleartext Storage in a File or on Disk\n- **Location:** `run-demo.sh:24-24`\n- **Vulnerable code:**\n\n  ```sh\n  # run-demo.sh:24-25, 72-74\n  JAR=\"$BUILD_DIR/demo-cookies.txt\"\n  rm -f \"$JAR\"\n  # …\n  show \"$(curl -sS -o /dev/null -D - -c \"$JAR\" -b \"$JAR\" -X POST \"$BASE_URL/login\" \\\n      -d 'username=alice' --data-urlencode 'password=Alice!2345' \\\n  ```\n\n- **Why it is a problem:** The script writes live credentials into the source tree. `JAR=\"$BUILD_DIR/demo-cookies.txt\"` (`:24`) is a Netscape cookie jar holding the session id in cleartext — the jar is deliberately re-read at `:58` to show what it contains — and `:52-53` additionally writes the raw response headers, `Set-Cookie` included, to `build/demo-login.hdr` and `build/demo-bad.hdr`. None of this is protected the way it would be in a browser: there is no `HttpOnly`, no expiry, and the files are ordinary mode-644 regular files that outlive the run, because `rm -f \"$JAR\"` at `:25` deletes the *previous* jar before the run, not the current one after it. Anyone who can read `build/` — a second user on the machine, a backup job, a container image layer, or a pasted directory listing — holds a bearer credential for `alice` until the server's idle timeout expires it. That is not hypothetical here: `build/demo-cookies.txt`, `build/demo-login.hdr` and `build/demo-bad.hdr` are present in this tree right now, so the artifacts of the last run are already sitting where they will be archived, committed, or attached to a bug report.
